[bradley2021:L195] , Data scarcity
[bradley2021:L196] \(\sqrt{(N-n)/n}\)
[bradley2021:L197] .
[bradley2021:L198] e
[bradley2021:L199] , Inherent problem difficulty
[bradley2021:L200] σ
[bradley2021:L201] Y
[bradley2021:L202] . Shaded bands represent scenarios of ±5% (darker) and ±10% (lighter) imprecision in the CDC benchmark relative to reported values (points).
[bradley2021:L203] b
[bradley2021:L204] –
[bradley2021:L205] e
[bradley2021:L206] comprise the decomposition in equation (
[bradley2021:L207] 1
[bradley2021:L208] ).
[bradley2021:L209] The Big Data Paradox in vaccine uptake
[bradley2021:L210] We focus on the Delphi–Facebook and Census Household Pulse surveys because their large sample sizes (each greater than 10,000 respondents
[bradley2021:L211] 20
[bradley2021:L212] ) present an opportunity to examine the Big Data Paradox
[bradley2021:L213] 1
[bradley2021:L214] in surveys. The Census Household Pulse is an experimental product designed to rapidly measure pandemic-related behaviour. Delphi–Facebook has stated that the intent of their survey is to make comparisons over space, time and subgroups, and that point estimates should be interpreted with caution
[bradley2021:L215] 3
[bradley2021:L216] . However, despite these intentions, Delphi–Facebook has reported point estimates of vaccine uptake in its own publications
[bradley2021:L217] 11
[bradley2021:L218] ,
[bradley2021:L219] 21
[bradley2021:L220] .
[bradley2021:L221] Delphi–Facebook and Census Household Pulse surveys persistently overestimate vaccine uptake relative to the CDC’s benchmark (Fig.
[bradley2021:L222] 1a
[bradley2021:L223] ) even taking into account Benchmark Imprecision (Fig. 1b) as explained in ‘Decomposing Error in COVID Surveys’. Despite being the smallest survey by an order of magnitude, the estimates of Axios–Ipsos track well with the CDC rates (Fig.
[bradley2021:L224] 1a
[bradley2021:L225] ), and their 95% confidence intervals contain the benchmark estimate from the CDC in 10 out of 11 surveys (an empirical coverage probability of 91%).
[bradley2021:L226] One might hope that estimates of changes in first-dose vaccine uptake are correct, even if each snapshot is biased. However, errors have increased over time, from just a few percentage points in January 2021 to Axios-Ipsos’ 4.2 percentage points [1–7 percentage points with 5% benchmark imprecision (BI)], Census Household Pulse’s 14 percentage points [5% BI: 11–17] and Delphi-Facebook’s 17 percentage points [5% BI: 14–20] by mid-May 2021 (Fig.
[bradley2021:L227] 1b
[bradley2021:L228] ). For context, for a state that is near the herd immunity threshold (70–80% based on recent estimates
[bradley2021:L229] 22
[bradley2021:L230] ), a discrepancy of 10 percentage points in vaccination rates could be the difference between containment and uncontrolled exponential growth in new SARS-CoV-2 infections.
[bradley2021:L231] Conventional statistical formulas for uncertainty further mislead when applied to biased big surveys because as sample size increases, bias (rather than variance) dominates estimator error. Figure
[bradley2021:L232] 1a
[bradley2021:L233] shows 95% confidence intervals for vaccine uptake based on the reported sampling standard errors and weighting design effects of each survey
[bradley2021:L234] 23
[bradley2021:L235] . Axios–Ipsos has the widest confidence intervals, but also the smallest design effects (1.1–1.2), suggesting that its accuracy is driven more by minimizing bias in data collection rather than post-survey adjustment. The 95% confidence intervals of Census Household Pulse are widened by large design effects (4.4–4.8) but they are still too narrow to include the true rate of vaccine uptake in almost all survey waves. The confidence intervals for Delphi–Facebook are extremely small, driven by large sample size and moderate design effects (1.4–1.5), and give us a negligible chance of being close to the truth.
[bradley2021:L236] One benefit of such large surveys might be to compare estimates of spatial and demographic subgroups
[bradley2021:L237] 24
[bradley2021:L238] ,
[bradley2021:L239] 25
[bradley2021:L240] ,
[bradley2021:L241] 26
[bradley2021:L242] . However, relative to the CDC’s contemporaneously reported state-level estimates, which did not include retroactive corrections, Delphi–Facebook and Census Household Pulse overestimated CDC state-level vaccine uptake by 16 and 9 percentage points, respectively (Extended Data Fig.
[bradley2021:L243] 1g, h
[bradley2021:L244] ) in March 2021, and by equal or larger amounts by May 2021 (Extended Data Fig.
[bradley2021:L245] 2g, h
[bradley2021:L246] ). Relative estimates were no better than absolute estimates in March of 2021: there is little agreement in a survey’s estimated state-level rankings with the CDC (a Kendall rank correlation of 0.31 for Delphi–Facebook in Extended Data Fig.
[bradley2021:L247] 1i
[bradley2021:L248] and 0.26 for Census Household Pulse in Extended Data Fig.
[bradley2021:L249] 1j
[bradley2021:L250] ) but they improved in May of 2021 (correlations of 0.78 and 0.74, respectively, in Extended Data Fig.
[bradley2021:L251] 2i, j
[bradley2021:L252] ). Among 18–64-year-olds, both Delphi–Facebook and Census Household Pulse overestimate uptake, with errors increasing over time (Extended Data Fig.
[bradley2021:L253] 6
[bradley2021:L254] ).
[bradley2021:L255] These examples illustrate a mathematical fact. That is, when biased samples are large, they are doubly misleading: they produce confidence intervals with incorrect centres and substantially underestimated widths. This is thev Big Data Paradox: “the bigger the data, the surer we fool ourselves”
[bradley2021:L256] 1
[bradley2021:L257] when we fail to account for bias in data collection.
[bradley2021:L258] A framework for quantifying data quality
[bradley2021:L259] Although it is well-understood that traditional confidence intervals capture only survey sampling errors
[bradley2021:L260] 27
[bradley2021:L261] (and not total error), the traditional survey framework lacks analytic tools for quantifying nonsampling errors separately from sampling errors. A previously formulated statistical framework
[bradley2021:L262] 1
[bradley2021:L263] permits us to exactly decompose the total error of a survey estimate into three components:
[bradley2021:L264] $$\begin{array}{c}{\rm{Total}}\,{\rm{error}}=\,{\rm{Data}}\,{\rm{quality}}\\ {\rm{defect}}\times {\rm{Data}}\,{\rm{scarcity}}\times {\rm{Inherent}}\,{\rm{problem}}\,{\rm{difficulty}}\end{array}$$
[bradley2021:L265] (1)
[bradley2021:L266] This framework has been applied to COVID-19 case counts
[bradley2021:L267] 28
[bradley2021:L268] and election forecasting
[bradley2021:L269] 29
[bradley2021:L270] . Its full application requires ground-truth benchmarks or their estimates from independent sources
[bradley2021:L271] 1
[bradley2021:L272] .
[bradley2021:L273] Specifically, the ‘total error’ is the difference between the observed sample mean
[bradley2021:L274] \({\overline{Y}}_{n}\)
[bradley2021:L275] as an estimator of the ground truth, the population mean
[bradley2021:L276] \({\overline{Y}}_{N}\)
[bradley2021:L277] . The ‘data quality defect’ is measured using
[bradley2021:L278] \({\hat{\rho }}_{Y,R}\)
[bradley2021:L279] , called the ‘data defect correlation’ (ddc)
[bradley2021:L280] 1
[bradley2021:L281] , which quantifies total bias (from any source), measured by the correlation between the event that an individual’s response is recorded and its value,
[bradley2021:L282] Y
[bradley2021:L283] . The effect of data quantity is captured by ‘data scarcity’, which is a function of the sample size
[bradley2021:L284] n
[bradley2021:L285] and the population size
[bradley2021:L286] N
[bradley2021:L287] , measured as
[bradley2021:L288] \(\sqrt{(N-n)/n},\)
[bradley2021:L289] and hence what matters for error is the relative sample size—that is, how close
[bradley2021:L290] n
[bradley2021:L291] is to
[bradley2021:L292] N
[bradley2021:L293] —rather than the absolute sample size
[bradley2021:L294] n
[bradley2021:L295] . The third factor is the ‘inherent problem difficulty’, which measures the population heterogeneity (via the standard deviation
[bradley2021:L296] σ
[bradley2021:L297] Y
[bradley2021:L298] of
[bradley2021:L299] Y
[bradley2021:L300] ), because the more heterogeneous a population is, the harder it is to estimate its average well. Mathematically, equation (
[bradley2021:L301] 1
[bradley2021:L302] ) is given by
[bradley2021:L303] \({\overline{Y}}_{n}-{\overline{Y}}_{N}={\hat{\rho }}_{Y,R}\times \sqrt{(N-n)/n}\times {\sigma }_{Y}\)
[bradley2021:L304] . This expression was inspired by the Hartley–Ross inequality for biases in ratio estimators
[bradley2021:L305] 30
[bradley2021:L306] . More details on the decomposition are provided in ‘Calculation and interpretation of ddc’ in the Methods, in which we also present a generalization for weighted estimators.
[bradley2021:L307] Decomposing error in COVID surveys
[bradley2021:L308] Although the ddc is not directly observed, COVID-19 surveys present a rare case in which it can be deduced because all of the other terms in equation (
[bradley2021:L309] 1
[bradley2021:L310] ) are known (see ‘Calculation and interpretation of ddc’ in the Methods for an in-depth explanation). We apply this framework to the aggregate error shown in Fig.
[bradley2021:L311] 1b
[bradley2021:L312] , and the resulting components of error from the right-hand side of equation (
[bradley2021:L313] 1
[bradley2021:L314] ) are shown in Fig.
[bradley2021:L315] 1c–e
[bradley2021:L316] .
[bradley2021:L317] We use the CDC’s report of the cumulative count of first doses administered to US adults as the benchmark
[bradley2021:L318] 8
[bradley2021:L319] ,
[bradley2021:L320] 13
[bradley2021:L321] ,
[bradley2021:L322] \({\overline{Y}}_{N}\)
[bradley2021:L323] . This benchmark time series may be affected by administrative delays and slippage in how the CDC centralizes information from states
[bradley2021:L324] 31
[bradley2021:L325] ,
[bradley2021:L326] 32
[bradley2021:L327] ,
[bradley2021:L328] 33
[bradley2021:L329] ,
[bradley2021:L330] 34
[bradley2021:L331] . The CDC continuously updates their entire time series retroactively for such delays as they are reported. But to account for potentially unreported delays, we present our results with Benchmark Imprecision (BI) in case the CDC’s numbers from our study period, 9 January to 26 May 2021, as reported on 26 May by the CDC suffer from ±5% and ±10% imprecision. These scenarios were chosen on the basis of analysis of the magnitude by which the CDC’s initial estimate for vaccine uptake by a particular day increases as the CDC receives delayed reports of vaccinations that occurred on that day (Extended Data Fig.
[bradley2021:L332] 3
[bradley2021:L333] ,
[bradley2021:L334] Supplementary Information A.2
[bradley2021:L335] ). That said, these scenarios may not capture latent systemic issues that affect CDC vaccination reporting.
[bradley2021:L336] The total error of each survey’s estimate of vaccine uptake (Fig.
[bradley2021:L337] 1b
[bradley2021:L338] ) increases over time for all studies, most markedly for Delphi–Facebook. The data quality defect, measured by the ddc, also increases over time for Census Household Pulse and for Delphi–Facebook (Fig.
[bradley2021:L339] 1c
[bradley2021:L340] ). The ddc for Axios–Ipsos is much smaller and steady over time, consistent with what one would expect from a representative sample. The data scarcity,
[bradley2021:L341] \(\sqrt{(N-n)/n},\)
[bradley2021:L342] for each survey is roughly constant across time (Fig.
[bradley2021:L343] 1d
[bradley2021:L344] ). Inherent problem difficulty is a population quantity common to all three surveys that peaks when the benchmark vaccination rate approaches 50% in April 2021 (Fig.
[bradley2021:L345] 1e
[bradley2021:L346] ). Therefore, the decomposition suggests that the increasing error in estimates of vaccine uptake in Delphi–Facebook and Census Household Pulse is primarily driven by increasing ddc, which captures the overall effect of the bias in coverage, selection and response.
[bradley2021:L347] Equation (
[bradley2021:L348] 1
[bradley2021:L349] ) also yields a formula for the bias-adjusted effective sample size
[bradley2021:L350] n
[bradley2021:L351] eff
[bradley2021:L352] , which is the size of a simple random sample that we would expect to exhibit the same level of mean squared error (MSE) as what was actually observed in a given study with a given ddc. Unlike the classical effective sample size
[bradley2021:L353] 23
[bradley2021:L354] , this quantity captures the effect of bias as well as that of an increase in variance from weighting and sampling. For details of this calculation, see ‘Error decomposition with survey weights’ in the Methods.
[bradley2021:L355] For estimating the US vaccination rate, Delphi–Facebook has a bias-adjusted effective sample size of less than 10 in April 2021, a 99.99% reduction from the raw average weekly sample size of 250,000 (Fig.
[bradley2021:L356] 2
[bradley2021:L357] ). The Census Household Pulse is also affected by over 99% reductions in effective sample size by May 2021. A simple random sample would have controlled estimation errors by controlling ddc. However, once this control is lost, small increases in ddc beyond what is expected in simple random samples can result in marked reductions of effective sample sizes for large populations
[bradley2021:L358] 1
[bradley2021:L359] .
[bradley2021:L360] Fig 2: Bias-adjusted effective sample size.
[bradley2021:L361] Full size image
[bradley2021:L362] An estimate’s bias-adjusted effective sample size (different from the classic Kish effective sample size) is the size of a simple random sample that would have the same MSE as the observed estimate. Effective sample sizes are shown here on the log
[bradley2021:L363] 10
[bradley2021:L364] scale. The original sample size was
[bradley2021:L365] n
[bradley2021:L366] = 4,525,633 across 19 waves for Delphi–Facebook,
[bradley2021:L367] n
[bradley2021:L368] = 606,615 across 8 waves for Census Household Pulse and
[bradley2021:L369] n
[bradley2021:L370] = 11,421 across 11 waves for Axios–Ipsos. Shaded bands represent scenarios of ±5% benchmark imprecision in the CDC benchmark.
[bradley2021:L371] Comparing study designs
[bradley2021:L372] Understanding why bias occurs in some surveys but not others requires an understanding of the sampling strategy, modes, questionnaire and weighting scheme of each survey. Table
[bradley2021:L373] 1
[bradley2021:L374] compares the design of each survey (more details in ‘Additional survey methodology’ in the Methods, Extended Data Table
[bradley2021:L375] 1
[bradley2021:L376] ).
[bradley2021:L377] All three surveys are conducted online and target the US adult population, but vary in the methods that they use to recruit respondents
[bradley2021:L378] 35
[bradley2021:L379] . The Delphi–Facebook survey recruits respondents from active Facebook users (the Facebook active user base, or FAUB) using daily unequal-probability stratified random sampling
[bradley2021:L380] 2
[bradley2021:L381] . The Census Bureau uses a systematic random sample to select households from the subset of the master address file (MAF) of the Census for which they have obtained either cell phone or email contact information (approximately 81% of all households in the MAF)
[bradley2021:L382] 4
[bradley2021:L383] .
[bradley2021:L384] In comparison, Axios–Ipsos relies on inverse response propensity sampling from Ipsos’ online KnowledgePanel. Ipsos recruits panellists using an address-based probabilistic sample from USPS’s delivery sequence file (DSF)
[bradley2021:L385] 5
[bradley2021:L386] . The DSF is similar to the MAF of the Census. Unlike the Census Household Pulse, potential respondents are not limited to the subset for whom email and phone contact information is available. Furthermore, Ipsos provides internet access and tablets to recruited panellists who lack home internet access. In 2021, this ‘offline’ group typically comprises 1% of the final survey (Extended Data Table
[bradley2021:L387] 1
[bradley2021:L388] ).
[bradley2021:L389] All three surveys weight on age and gender; that is, assign larger weights to respondents of underrepresented age by gender subgroups and smaller weights to those of overrepresented subgroups
[bradley2021:L390] 2
[bradley2021:L391] ,
[bradley2021:L392] 4
[bradley2021:L393] ,
[bradley2021:L394] 5
[bradley2021:L395] (Table
[bradley2021:L396] 1
[bradley2021:L397] ). Axios–Ipsos and Census Household Pulse also weight on education and race and/or ethnicity (hereafter, race/ethnicity). Axios–Ipsos additionally weights to the composition of political partisanship measured by “recent ABC News/Washington Post telephone polls”
[bradley2021:L398] 5
[bradley2021:L399] in 6 of the 11 waves we study. Education—a known correlate of propensity to respond to surveys
[bradley2021:L400] 36
[bradley2021:L401] and social media use
[bradley2021:L402] 37
[bradley2021:L403] are notably absent from Delphi–Facebook’s weighting scheme, as is race/ethnicity. As noted before, none of the surveys use the CDC benchmark to adjust or assess estimates of vaccine uptake.
[bradley2021:L404] Explanations for error
[bradley2021:L405] Table
[bradley2021:L406] 2
[bradley2021:L407] illustrates some consequences of these design choices. Axios–Ipsos samples mimic the actual breakdown of education attainment among US adults even before weighting, whereas those of Census Household Pulse and Delphi–Facebook do not. After weighting, Axios–Ipsos and Census Household Pulse match the population benchmark, by design. Delphi–Facebook does not explicitly weight on education, and hence the education bias persists in their weighted estimates: those without a college degree are underrepresented by nearly 20 percentage points. The case is similar for race/ethnicity. Delphi–Facebook’s weighting scheme does not adjust for race/ethnicity, and hence their weighted sample still overrepresents white adults by 8 percentage points, and underrepresents the proportions of Black and Asian individuals by around 50% of their size in the population (Table
[bradley2021:L408] 2
[bradley2021:L409] ).
[bradley2021:L410] Table 2 Composition of survey respondents by educational attainment and race/ethnicity
[bradley2021:L411] Full size table
[bradley2021:L412] The overrepresentation of white adults and people with college degrees explains part of the error of Delphi–Facebook. The racial groups that Delphi–Facebook underrepresents tend to be more willing and less vaccinated in the samples (Table
[bradley2021:L413] 2
[bradley2021:L414] ). In other words, reweighting the Delphi–Facebook survey to upweight racial minorities will bring willingness estimates closer to Household Pulse and the vaccination rate closer to CDC. The three surveys also report that people without a four-year college degree are less likely to have been vaccinated compared to those with a degree (Table
[bradley2021:L415] 2
[bradley2021:L416] , Supplementary Table
[bradley2021:L417] 1
[bradley2021:L418] ). If we assume that vaccination behaviours do not differ systematically between non-respondents and respondents within each demographic category, underrepresentation of less-vaccinated groups would contribute to the bias found here. However, this alone cannot explain the discrepancies in all the outcomes. Census Household Pulse weights on both race and education
[bradley2021:L419] 4
[bradley2021:L420] and still overestimates vaccine uptake by over ten points in late May of 2021 (Fig.
[bradley2021:L421] 1b
[bradley2021:L422] ).
[bradley2021:L423] Delphi–Facebook and Census Household Pulse may be unrepresentative with respect to political partisanship, which has been found to be correlated with vaccine behaviour
[bradley2021:L424] 38
[bradley2021:L425] and with survey response
[bradley2021:L426] 39
[bradley2021:L427] , and thus may contribute to observed bias. However, neither Delphi–Facebook nor Census Household Pulse collects partisanship of respondents. US Census agencies cannot ask about political preference, and no unequivocal population benchmark for partisanship in the general adult population exists.
[bradley2021:L428] Rurality may also contribute to the errors, because it correlates with vaccine status
[bradley2021:L429] 8
[bradley2021:L430] and home internet access
[bradley2021:L431] 40
[bradley2021:L432] . Neither Census Household Pulse nor Delphi–Facebook weights on sub-state geography, which may mean that adults in more rural areas who are less likely to be vaccinated are also underrepresented in the two surveys, leading to overestimation of vaccine uptake.
[bradley2021:L433] Axios–Ipsos weights to metropolitan status and also recruits a fraction of its panellists from an ‘offline’ population of individuals without internet access. We find that dropping these offline respondents (
[bradley2021:L434] n
[bradley2021:L435] = 21, or 1% of the sample) in their 22 March 2021 wave increases Axios–Ipsos’ overall estimate of the vaccination rate by 0.5 percentage points, thereby increasing the total error (Extended Data Table
[bradley2021:L436] 2
[bradley2021:L437] ). However, this offline population is too small to explain the entirety of the difference in accuracy between Axios–Ipsos and either Census Household Pulse (6 percentage points) or Delphi–Facebook (14 percentage points), in this time period.
[bradley2021:L438] Careful recruitment of panellists is at least as important as weighting. Weighting on observed covariates alone cannot explain or correct the discrepancies we observe. For example, reweighting Axios–Ipsos 22 March 2021 wave using only Delphi–Facebook’s weighting variables (age group and gender) increased the error in their vaccination estimates by 1 percentage point, but this estimate with Axios–Ipsos data is still more accurate than that from Delphi–Facebook during the same period (Extended Data Table
[bradley2021:L439] 2
[bradley2021:L440] ). The Axios–Ipsos estimate with Delphi–Facebook weighting overestimated vaccination by 2 percentage points, whereas Delphi–Facebook overestimated it by 11 percentage points.
[bradley2021:L441] The key implication is that there is no silver bullet: every small part of panel recruitment, sampling and weighting matters for controlling the data quality measured as the correlation between an outcome and response—what we call the ddc. In multi-stage sampling, which includes for example the selection of potential respondents followed by non-response, bias in even a single step can substantially affect the final result (‘Population size in multi-stage sampling’ in the
[bradley2021:L442] Methods
[bradley2021:L443] , Extended Data Table
[bradley2021:L444] 3
[bradley2021:L445] ). A total quality control approach, inspired by the total survey error framework
[bradley2021:L446] 41
[bradley2021:L447] , is a better strategy than trying to prioritize some components over others to improve data quality. This emphasis is a reaffirmation of the best practice for survey research as advocated by the American Association for Public Opinion Research:
[bradley2021:L448] 6
[bradley2021:L449] “The quality of a survey is best judged not by its size, scope, or prominence, but by how much attention is given to [preventing, measuring and] dealing with the many important problems that can arise.”
[bradley2021:L450] 42
[bradley2021:L451] Addressing common misperceptions
[bradley2021:L452] The three surveys discussed in this article demonstrate a seemingly paradoxical phenomenon—the two larger surveys that we studied are more statistically confident, but also more biased, than the smaller, more traditional Axios–Ipsos poll. These findings are paradoxical only when we fall into the trap of the intuition that estimation errors necessarily decrease in larger datasets
[bradley2021:L453] 12
[bradley2021:L454] .
[bradley2021:L455] A limitation of our vaccine uptake analysis is that we only examine ddc with respect to an outcome for which a benchmark is available: first-dose vaccine uptake. One might hope that surveys biased on vaccine uptake are not biased on other outcomes, for which there may not be benchmarks to reveal their biases. However, the absence of evidence of bias for the remaining outcomes is not evidence of its absence. In fact, mathematically, when a survey is found to be biased with respect to one variable, it implies that the entire survey fails to be statistically representative. The theory of survey sampling relies on statistical representativeness for all variables achieved through probabilistic sampling
[bradley2021:L456] 43
[bradley2021:L457] . Indeed, Neyman’s original introduction of probabilistic sampling showed the limits of purposive sampling, which attempted to achieve overall representativeness by enforcing it only on a set of variables
[bradley2021:L458] 18
[bradley2021:L459] ,
[bradley2021:L460] 44
[bradley2021:L461] .
[bradley2021:L462] In other words, when a survey loses its overall statistical representativeness (for example, through bias in coverage or non-response), which is difficult to repair (for example, by weighting or modelling on observable characteristics) and almost impossible to verify
[bradley2021:L463] 45
[bradley2021:L464] , researchers who wish to use the survey for scientific studies must supply other reasons to justify the reliability of their survey estimates, such as evidence about the independence between the variable of interest and the factors that are responsible for the unrepresentativeness. Furthermore, scientific journals that publish studies based on surveys that may be unrepresentative
[bradley2021:L465] 17
[bradley2021:L466] —especially those with large sizes such as Delphi–Facebook (biased with respect to vaccination status (Fig.
[bradley2021:L467] 1
[bradley2021:L468] ), race and education (Table
[bradley2021:L469] 2
[bradley2021:L470] ))—need to ask for reasonable effort from the authors to address the unrepresentativeness.
[bradley2021:L471] Some may argue that bias is a necessary trade-off for having data that are sufficiently large for conducting highly granular analysis, such as county-level estimation of vaccine hesitancy
[bradley2021:L472] 26
[bradley2021:L473] . Although high-resolution inference is important, we warn that this is a double-edged argument. A highly biased estimate with a misleadingly small confidence interval can do more damage than having no estimate at all. We further note that bias is not limited to population point estimates, but also affects estimates of changes over time (contrary to published guidance
[bradley2021:L474] 3
[bradley2021:L475] ). Both Delphi–Facebook and Census Household Pulse significantly overestimate the slope of vaccine uptake relative to that of the CDC benchmark (Fig.
[bradley2021:L476] 1b
[bradley2021:L477] ).
[bradley2021:L478] The accuracy of our analysis does rely on the accuracy of the CDC’s estimates of COVID vaccine uptake. However, if the selection bias in the CDC’s benchmark is significant enough to alter our results, then that itself would be another example of the Big Data Paradox.
[bradley2021:L479] Discussion
[bradley2021:L480] This is not the first time that the Big Data Paradox has appeared: Google Trends predicted more than twice the number of influenza-like illnesses than the CDC in February 2013
[bradley2021:L481] 46
[bradley2021:L482] . This analysis demonstrates that the Big Data Paradox applies not only to organically collected Big Data, like Google Trends, but also to surveys. Delphi–Facebook is “the largest public health survey ever conducted in the United States”
[bradley2021:L483] 47
[bradley2021:L484] . The Census Household Pulse is conducted in collaboration between the US Census Bureau and eleven statistical government partners, all with enormous resources and survey expertise. Both studies take steps to mitigate selection bias, but substantially overestimate vaccine uptake. As we have shown, the effect of bias is magnified as relative sample size increases.
[bradley2021:L485] By contrast, Axios–Ipsos records only about 1,000 responses per wave, but makes additional efforts to prevent selection bias. Small surveys can be just as wrong as large surveys in expectation—of the three other small-to-medium online surveys additionally analysed, two also miss the CDC vaccination benchmark (Extended Data Fig.
[bradley2021:L486] 5
[bradley2021:L487] ). The overall lesson is that investing in data quality (particularly during collection, but also in analysis) minimizes error more efficiently than does increasing data quantity. Of course, a sample size of 1,000 may be too small (that is, leading to unhelpfully large uncertainty intervals) for the kind of 50-state analyses made possible by big surveys. However, small-area methods that borrow information across subgroups
[bradley2021:L488] 48
[bradley2021:L489] can perform better with higher-quality—albeit few—data, and whether that approach would outperform the large, biased surveys is an open question.
[bradley2021:L490] There are approaches to correct for these biases in both probability and nonprobability samples alike. For COVID-19 surveys in particular, since June 2021, the AP–NORC multimode panel has weighted their COVID-19 related surveys to the CDC benchmark, so that the weighted ddc for vaccine uptake is zero by design
[bradley2021:L491] 49
[bradley2021:L492] . More generally, there is an extensive literature on approaches for making inferences from data collected from nonprobability samples
[bradley2021:L493] 50
[bradley2021:L494] ,
[bradley2021:L495] 51
[bradley2021:L496] ,
[bradley2021:L497] 52
[bradley2021:L498] . Other promising approaches include integrating surveys of varying quality
[bradley2021:L499] 53
[bradley2021:L500] ,
[bradley2021:L501] 54
[bradley2021:L502] , and leveraging the estimated ddc in one outcome to correct bias in others under several scenarios (
[bradley2021:L503] Supplementary Information D
[bradley2021:L504] ).
[bradley2021:L505] Although more needs to be done to fully examine the nuances of large surveys, organically collected administrative datasets and social media data, we hope that this comparative study of ddc highlights the concerning implications of the Big Data Paradox—how large sample sizes magnify the effect of seemingly small defects in data collection, which leads to overconfidence in incorrect inferences.
[bradley2021:L506] Methods
[bradley2021:L507] Calculation and interpretation of ddc
[bradley2021:L508] The mathematical expression for equation (
[bradley2021:L509] 1
[bradley2021:L510] ) is given here for completeness:
[bradley2021:L511] $${\overline{Y}}_{n}-{\overline{Y}}_{N}={\hat{\rho }}_{Y,R}\times \sqrt{\frac{N-n}{n}}\times {\sigma }_{Y}$$
[bradley2021:L512] (2)
[bradley2021:L513] The first factor
[bradley2021:L514] \({\hat{\rho }}_{Y,R}\)
[bradley2021:L515] is called the data defect correlation (ddc)
[bradley2021:L516] 1
[bradley2021:L517] . It is a measure of data quality represented by the correlation between the recording indicator
[bradley2021:L518] R
[bradley2021:L519] (
[bradley2021:L520] R
[bradley2021:L521] = 1 if an answer is recorded and
[bradley2021:L522] R
[bradley2021:L523] = 0 otherwise) and its value,
[bradley2021:L524] Y
[bradley2021:L525] . Given a benchmark, the ddc
[bradley2021:L526] \({\hat{\rho }}_{Y,R}\)
[bradley2021:L527] can be calculated by substituting known quantities into equation (
[bradley2021:L528] 2
[bradley2021:L529] ). In the case of a single survey wave of a COVID-19 survey,
[bradley2021:L530] n
[bradley2021:L531] is the sample size of the survey wave,
[bradley2021:L532] N
[bradley2021:L533] is the population size of US adults from US Census estimates
[bradley2021:L534] 55
[bradley2021:L535] ,
[bradley2021:L536] \({\overline{Y}}_{n}\)
[bradley2021:L537] is the survey estimate of vaccine uptake and
[bradley2021:L538] \({\overline{Y}}_{N}\)
[bradley2021:L539] is the estimate of vaccine uptake for the corresponding period taken from the CDC’s report of the cumulative count of first doses administered to US adults
[bradley2021:L540] 8
[bradley2021:L541] ,
[bradley2021:L542] 13
[bradley2021:L543] . We calculate
[bradley2021:L544] \({\sigma }_{Y}=\sqrt{{\overline{Y}}_{N}(1-{\overline{Y}}_{N})}\)
[bradley2021:L545] because
[bradley2021:L546] Y
[bradley2021:L547] is binary (but equation (
[bradley2021:L548] 2
[bradley2021:L549] ) is not restricted to binary
[bradley2021:L550] Y
[bradley2021:L551] ).
[bradley2021:L552] We calculate
[bradley2021:L553] \({\hat{\rho }}_{Y,R}\)
[bradley2021:L554] by using total error
[bradley2021:L555] \({\overline{Y}}_{n}-{\overline{Y}}_{N}\)
[bradley2021:L556] , which captures not only selection bias but also any measurement bias (for example, from question wording). However, with this calculation method,
[bradley2021:L557] \({\hat{\rho }}_{Y,R}\)
[bradley2021:L558] lacks the direct interpretation as a correlation between
[bradley2021:L559] Y
[bradley2021:L560] and
[bradley2021:L561] R
[bradley2021:L562] , and instead becomes a more general index of data quality directly related to classical design effects (see ‘Bias-adjusted effective sample size’).
[bradley2021:L563] It is important to point out that the increase in ddc does not necessarily imply that the response mechanisms for Delphi–Facebook and Census Household Pulse have changed over time. The correlation between a changing outcome and a steady response mechanism could change over time, hence changing the value of ddc. For example, as more individuals become vaccinated, and vaccination status is driven by individual behaviour rather than eligibility, the correlation between vaccination status and propensity to respond could increase even if the propensity to respond for a given individual is constant. This would lead to large values of ddc over time, reflecting the increased impact of the same response mechanism.
[bradley2021:L564] Error decomposition with survey weights
[bradley2021:L565] The data quality framework given by equations (
[bradley2021:L566] 1
[bradley2021:L567] ) and (
[bradley2021:L568] 2
[bradley2021:L569] ) is a special case of a more general framework for assessing the actual error of a weighted estimator
[bradley2021:L570] \({\overline{Y}}_{w}=\frac{{\sum }_{i}{w}_{i}{R}_{i}{Y}_{i}}{{\sum }_{i}{w}_{i}{R}_{i}}\)
[bradley2021:L571] , where
[bradley2021:L572] \({w}_{i}\)
[bradley2021:L573] is the survey weight assigned to individual
[bradley2021:L574] \(i\)
[bradley2021:L575] . It is shown in Meng
[bradley2021:L576] 1
[bradley2021:L577] that
[bradley2021:L578] $${\overline{Y}}_{{\rm{w}}}-{\overline{Y}}_{N}={\hat{\rho }}_{Y,{R}_{{\rm{w}}}}\times \sqrt{\frac{N-{n}_{{\rm{w}}}}{{n}_{{\rm{w}}}}}\times {\sigma }_{Y},$$
[bradley2021:L579] (3)
[bradley2021:L580] where
[bradley2021:L581] \({\hat{\rho }}_{Y,{R}_{{\rm{w}}}}={\rm{Corr}}(Y,{R}_{{\rm{w}}})\)
[bradley2021:L582] is the finite population correlation between
[bradley2021:L583] \({Y}_{i}\)
[bradley2021:L584] and
[bradley2021:L585] \({R}_{{\rm{w}},i}={w}_{i}{R}_{i}\)
[bradley2021:L586] (over
[bradley2021:L587] i
[bradley2021:L588] = 1, …,
[bradley2021:L589] N
[bradley2021:L590] ). The ‘hat’ on
[bradley2021:L591] ρ
[bradley2021:L592] reminds us that this correlation depends on the specific realization of {
[bradley2021:L593] R
[bradley2021:L594] i
[bradley2021:L595] ,
[bradley2021:L596] i
[bradley2021:L597] = 1, …,
[bradley2021:L598] N
[bradley2021:L599] }. The term
[bradley2021:L600] n
[bradley2021:L601] w
[bradley2021:L602] is the classical ‘effective sample size’ due to weighting
[bradley2021:L603] 23
[bradley2021:L604] ; that is,
[bradley2021:L605] \({n}_{{\rm{w}}}=\frac{n}{(1+{{\rm{CV}}}_{{\rm{w}}}^{2})}\)
[bradley2021:L606] , where CV
[bradley2021:L607] w
[bradley2021:L608] is the coefficient of variation of the weights for all individuals in the observed sample, that is, the standard deviation of weights normalized by their mean. It is common for surveys to rescale their weights to have mean 1, in which case
[bradley2021:L609] \({{\rm{CV}}}_{w}^{2}\)
[bradley2021:L610] is simply the sample variance of
[bradley2021:L611] W
[bradley2021:L612] .
[bradley2021:L613] When all weights are the same, equation (
[bradley2021:L614] 3
[bradley2021:L615] ) reduces to equation (
[bradley2021:L616] 2
[bradley2021:L617] ). In other words, the ddc term
[bradley2021:L618] \({\hat{\rho }}_{Y,{R}_{{\rm{w}}}}\)
[bradley2021:L619] now also takes into account the effect of the weights as a means to combat the selection bias represented by the recording indicator
[bradley2021:L620] R
[bradley2021:L621] . Intuitively, if
[bradley2021:L622] \({\hat{\rho }}_{Y,R}={\rm{Corr}}(Y,R)\)
[bradley2021:L623] is high (in magnitude), then some
[bradley2021:L624] Y
[bradley2021:L625] i
[bradley2021:L626] ’s have a higher chance of entering our dataset than others, thus leading to a sample average that is a biased estimator for the population average. Incorporating appropriate weights can reduce
[bradley2021:L627] \({\hat{\rho }}_{Y,R}\)
[bradley2021:L628] to
[bradley2021:L629] \({\hat{\rho }}_{Y,{R}_{{\rm{w}}}}\)
[bradley2021:L630] , with the aim of reducing the effect of the selection bias. However, this reduction alone may not be sufficient to improve the accuracy of
[bradley2021:L631] \({\overline{Y}}_{w}\)
[bradley2021:L632] because the use of weight necessarily reduces the sampling fraction
[bradley2021:L633] \(f=\frac{n}{N}\)
[bradley2021:L634] to
[bradley2021:L635] \({f}_{{\rm{w}}}=\frac{{n}_{{\rm{w}}}}{N}\)
[bradley2021:L636] as well, as
[bradley2021:L637] n
[bradley2021:L638] w
[bradley2021:L639] <
[bradley2021:L640] n
[bradley2021:L641] . Equation (
[bradley2021:L642] 3
[bradley2021:L643] ) precisely describes this trade-off, providing a formula to assess when the reduction of ddc is significant to outweigh the reduction of the effective sample size.
[bradley2021:L644] Measuring the correlation between
[bradley2021:L645] Y
[bradley2021:L646] and
[bradley2021:L647] R
[bradley2021:L648] is not a new idea in survey statistics (though note that ddc is the population correlation between
[bradley2021:L649] Y
[bradley2021:L650] and
[bradley2021:L651] R
[bradley2021:L652] , not the sample correlation), nor is the observation that as sample size increases, error is dominated by bias instead of variance
[bradley2021:L653] 56
[bradley2021:L654] ,
[bradley2021:L655] 57
[bradley2021:L656] . The new insight is that ddc is a general metric to index the lack of representativeness of the data we observe, regardless of whether or not the sample is obtained through a probabilistic scheme, or weighted to mimic a probabilistic sample. As discussed in ‘Addressing common misperceptions’ in the main text, any single ddc deviating from what is expected under representative sampling (for example, probabilistic sampling) is sufficient to establish that the sample is not representative (but the converse is not true). Furthermore, the ddc framework refutes the common belief that increasing sample size necessarily improves statistical estimation
[bradley2021:L657] 1
[bradley2021:L658] ,
[bradley2021:L659] 58
[bradley2021:L660] .
[bradley2021:L661] Bias-adjusted effective sample size
[bradley2021:L662] By matching the mean-squared error of
[bradley2021:L663] \({\overline{Y}}_{w}\)
[bradley2021:L664] with the variance of the sample average from simple random sampling, Meng
[bradley2021:L665] 1
[bradley2021:L666] derives the following formula for calculating a bias-adjusted effective sample size, or
[bradley2021:L667] n
[bradley2021:L668] eff
[bradley2021:L669] :
[bradley2021:L670] $$\begin{array}{r}{n}_{{\rm{eff}}}=\frac{{n}_{{\rm{w}}}}{N-{n}_{{\rm{w}}}}\times \frac{1}{E[{\hat{\rho }}_{Y,{R}_{{\rm{w}}}}^{2}]}\end{array}$$
[bradley2021:L671] Given an estimator
[bradley2021:L672] \({\overline{Y}}_{w}\)
[bradley2021:L673] with expected total MSE
[bradley2021:L674] T
[bradley2021:L675] due to data defect, sampling variability and weighting, this quantity
[bradley2021:L676] n
[bradley2021:L677] eff
[bradley2021:L678] represents the size of a simple random sample such that its mean
[bradley2021:L679] \({\bar{Y}}_{N}\)
[bradley2021:L680] , as an estimator for the same population mean
[bradley2021:L681] \({\overline{Y}}_{N}\)
[bradley2021:L682] , would have the identical MSE
[bradley2021:L683] T
[bradley2021:L684] . The term
[bradley2021:L685] \(E[{\hat{\rho }}_{Y,{R}_{{\rm{w}}}}^{2}]\)
[bradley2021:L686] represents the amount of selection bias (squared) expected on average from a particular recording mechanism
[bradley2021:L687] R
[bradley2021:L688] and a chosen weighting scheme.
[bradley2021:L689] For each survey wave, we use
[bradley2021:L690] \({\hat{\rho }}_{Y,{R}_{{\rm{w}}}}^{2}\)
[bradley2021:L691] to approximate
[bradley2021:L692] \(E[{\hat{\rho }}_{Y,{R}_{{\rm{w}}}}^{2}]\)
[bradley2021:L693] . This estimation is unbiased by design, as we use an estimator to estimate its expectation. Therefore, the only source of error is the sampling variation, which is typically negligible for large surveys such as Delphi–Facebook and the Census Household Pulse. This estimation error may have more impact for smaller surveys such as the Axios–Ipsos survey, an issue that we will investigate in subsequent work.
[bradley2021:L694] We compute
[bradley2021:L695] \({\hat{\rho }}_{Y,{R}_{{\rm{w}}}}\)
[bradley2021:L696] by using the benchmark
[bradley2021:L697] \({\overline{Y}}_{N}\)
[bradley2021:L698] , namely, by solving equation (
[bradley2021:L699] 3
[bradley2021:L700] ) for
[bradley2021:L701] \({\hat{\rho }}_{Y,{R}_{{\rm{w}}}}\)
[bradley2021:L702] ,
[bradley2021:L703] $${\hat{\rho }}_{Y,{R}_{{\rm{w}}}}=\frac{{Z}_{{\rm{w}}}}{\sqrt{N}},{\rm{where}}\,{Z}_{{\rm{w}}}=\frac{{\overline{Y}}_{{\rm{w}}}-{\overline{Y}}_{N}}{\sqrt{\frac{1-{f}_{{\rm{w}}}}{{n}_{{\rm{w}}}}}{\sigma }_{Y}}$$
[bradley2021:L704] We introduce this notation
[bradley2021:L705] Z
[bradley2021:L706] w
[bradley2021:L707] because it is the quantity that determines the well-known survey efficiency measure, the so-called ‘design effect’, which is the variance of
[bradley2021:L708] Z
[bradley2021:L709] w
[bradley2021:L710] for a probabilistic sampling design
[bradley2021:L711] 23
[bradley2021:L712] (when we assume the weights are fixed). For the more general setting in which
[bradley2021:L713] \({\overline{Y}}_{w}\)
[bradley2021:L714] may be biased, we replace the variance by MSE, and hence the bias-adjusted design effect
[bradley2021:L715] \({D}_{e}=E[{Z}_{{\rm{w}}}^{2}]\)
[bradley2021:L716] , which is the MSE relative to the benchmark measured in the unit of the variance of an average from a simple random sample of size
[bradley2021:L717] n
[bradley2021:L718] w
[bradley2021:L719] . Hence
[bradley2021:L720] \({D}_{I}\equiv E[{\hat{\rho }}_{Y,{R}_{{\rm{w}}}}^{2}]\)
[bradley2021:L721] , which was termed as ‘data defect index’
[bradley2021:L722] 1
[bradley2021:L723] , is simply the bias-adjusted design effect per unit, because
[bradley2021:L724] \({D}_{I}=\frac{{D}_{e}}{N}\)
[bradley2021:L725] .
[bradley2021:L726] Furthermore, because
[bradley2021:L727] \({Z}_{{\rm{w}}}\)
[bradley2021:L728] is the standardized actual error, it captures any kind of error inherited in
[bradley2021:L729] \({\overline{Y}}_{w}\)
[bradley2021:L730] . This observation is important because when
[bradley2021:L731] Y
[bradley2021:L732] is subject to measurement errors,
[bradley2021:L733] \(\frac{{Z}_{{\rm{w}}}}{\sqrt{N}}\)
[bradley2021:L734] no longer has the simple interpretation as a correlation. But because we estimate
[bradley2021:L735] \({D}_{I}\)
[bradley2021:L736] by
[bradley2021:L737] \(\frac{{Z}_{w}^{2}}{N}\)
[bradley2021:L738] directly, our effective sample size calculation is still valid even when equation (
[bradley2021:L739] 3
[bradley2021:L740] ) does not hold.
[bradley2021:L741] Asymptotic behaviour of ddc
[bradley2021:L742] As shown in Meng
[bradley2021:L743] 1
[bradley2021:L744] , for any probabilistic sample without selection biases, the ddc is on the order of
[bradley2021:L745] \(\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\)
[bradley2021:L746] . Hence the magnitude of
[bradley2021:L747] \({\hat{\rho }}_{Y,R}\)
[bradley2021:L748] (or
[bradley2021:L749] \({\hat{\rho }}_{Y,{R}_{{\rm{w}}}})\)
[bradley2021:L750] is small enough to cancel out the effect of
[bradley2021:L751] \(\sqrt{N-n}\)
[bradley2021:L752] (or
[bradley2021:L753] \(\sqrt{N-{n}_{{\rm{w}}}}\)
[bradley2021:L754] ) in the data scarcity term on the actual error, as seen in equation (
[bradley2021:L755] 2
[bradley2021:L756] ) (or equation (
[bradley2021:L757] 3
[bradley2021:L758] )). However, when a sample is unrepresentative; for example, when those with
[bradley2021:L759] Y
[bradley2021:L760] = 1 are more likely to enter the dataset than those with
[bradley2021:L761] Y
[bradley2021:L762] = 0, then
[bradley2021:L763] \({\hat{\rho }}_{Y,R}\)
[bradley2021:L764] can far exceed
[bradley2021:L765] \(\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\)
[bradley2021:L766] in magnitude. In this case, error will increase with
[bradley2021:L767] \(\sqrt{N}\)
[bradley2021:L768] for a fixed ddc and growing population size
[bradley2021:L769] N
[bradley2021:L770] (equation (
[bradley2021:L771] 2
[bradley2021:L772] )). This result may be counterintuitive in the traditional survey statistics framework, which often considers how error changes as sample size
[bradley2021:L773] n
[bradley2021:L774] grows. The ddc framework considers a more general set-up, taking into account individual response behaviour, including its effect on sample size itself.
[bradley2021:L775] As an example of how response behaviour can shape both total error and the number of respondents
[bradley2021:L776] n
[bradley2021:L777] , suppose individual response behaviour is captured by a logistic regression model
[bradley2021:L778] $${\rm{logit}}[{\rm{\Pr }}(R=1|Y)]=\alpha +\beta Y.$$
[bradley2021:L779] (4)
[bradley2021:L780] This is a model for a response propensity score. Its value is determined by
[bradley2021:L781] α
[bradley2021:L782] , which drives the overall sampling fraction
[bradley2021:L783] \(f=\frac{n}{N}\)
[bradley2021:L784] , and by
[bradley2021:L785] β
[bradley2021:L786] , which controls how strongly
[bradley2021:L787] Y
[bradley2021:L788] influences whether a participant will respond or not.
[bradley2021:L789] In this logit response model, when
[bradley2021:L790] \(\beta \ne 0\)
[bradley2021:L791] ,
[bradley2021:L792] \({\hat{\rho }}_{Y,R}\)
[bradley2021:L793] is determined by individual behaviour, not by population size
[bradley2021:L794] N
[bradley2021:L795] . In
[bradley2021:L796] Supplementary Information B.1
[bradley2021:L797] , we prove that ddc cannot vanish as
[bradley2021:L798] N
[bradley2021:L799] grows, nor can the observed sample size
[bradley2021:L800] n
[bradley2021:L801] ever approach 0 or
[bradley2021:L802] N
[bradley2021:L803] for a given set of (finite and plausible) values of {α, β}, because there will always be a non-trivial percentage of non-respondents. For example, an
[bradley2021:L804] f
[bradley2021:L805] of 0.01 can be obtained under this model for either
[bradley2021:L806] α
[bradley2021:L807] = −0.46,
[bradley2021:L808] β
[bradley2021:L809] = 0 (no influence of individual behaviour on response propensity), or for
[bradley2021:L810] α
[bradley2021:L811] = −3.9,
[bradley2021:L812] β
[bradley2021:L813] = −4.84. However, despite the same
[bradley2021:L814] f
[bradley2021:L815] , the implied ddc and consequently the MSE will differ. For example, the MSE for the former (no correlation with
[bradley2021:L816] Y
[bradley2021:L817] ) is 0.0004, whereas the MSE for the latter (a −4.84 coefficient on
[bradley2021:L818] Y
[bradley2021:L819] ) is 0.242, over 600 times larger.
[bradley2021:L820] See
[bradley2021:L821] Supplementary Information B.2
[bradley2021:L822] for the connection between ddc and a well-studied non-response model from econometrics, the Heckman selection model
[bradley2021:L823] 59
[bradley2021:L824] .
[bradley2021:L825] Population size in multi-stage sampling
[bradley2021:L826] We have shown that the asymptotic behaviour of error depends on whether the data collection process is driven by individual response behaviour or by survey design. The reality is often a mix of both. Consequently, the relevant ‘population size’
[bradley2021:L827] N
[bradley2021:L828] depends on when and where the representativeness of the sample is destroyed; that is, when the individual response behaviours come into play. Real-world surveys that are as complex as the three surveys we analyse here have multiple stages of sample selection.
[bradley2021:L829] Extended Data Table
[bradley2021:L830] 3
[bradley2021:L831] takes as an example the sampling stages of the Census Household Pulse, which has the most extensive set of documentation among the three surveys we analyse. As we have summarized (Table
[bradley2021:L832] 1
[bradley2021:L833] , Extended Data Table
[bradley2021:L834] 1
[bradley2021:L835] ), the Census Household Pulse (1) first defines the sampling frame as the reachable subset of the MAF, (2) takes a random sample of that population to prompt (send a survey questionnaire) and (3) waits for individuals to respond to that survey. Each of these stages reduces the desired data size, and the corresponding population size is the intended sample size from the prior stage (in notation,
[bradley2021:L836] N
[bradley2021:L837] s
[bradley2021:L838] =
[bradley2021:L839] n
[bradley2021:L840] s
[bradley2021:L841] −1
[bradley2021:L842] , for
[bradley2021:L843] s
[bradley2021:L844] = 2, 3). For example, in stage 3, the population size
[bradley2021:L845] N
[bradley2021:L846] 3
[bradley2021:L847] is the size of the intended sample size
[bradley2021:L848] n
[bradley2021:L849] 2
[bradley2021:L850] from the second stage (random sample of the outreach list), because only the sampled individuals have a chance to respond.
[bradley2021:L851] Although all stages contribute to the overall ddc, the stage that dominates is the first stage at which the representativeness of our sample is destroyed—the size of which will be labelled as the dominating population size (dps)—when the relevant population size decreases markedly at each step. However, we must bear in mind that dps refers to the worst-case scenario, when biases accumulate, instead of (accidentally) cancelling each other out.
[bradley2021:L852] For example, if the 20% of the MAFs excluded from the Census Household Pulse sampling frame (because they had no cell phone or email contact information) is not representative of the US adult population, then the dps is
[bradley2021:L853] N
[bradley2021:L854] 1
[bradley2021:L855] , or 255 million adults contained in 144 million households. Then the increase in bias for given ddc is driven by the rate of
[bradley2021:L856] \(\sqrt{{N}_{1}}\)
[bradley2021:L857] where
[bradley2021:L858] N
[bradley2021:L859] 1
[bradley2021:L860] = 2.55 × 10
[bradley2021:L861] 8
[bradley2021:L862] and is large indeed (with
[bradley2021:L863] \(\sqrt{2.5\times {10}^{8}}\approx \mathrm{15,000}\)
[bradley2021:L864] ). By contrast, if the the sampling frame is representative of the target population and the outreach list is representative of the frame (and hence representative of the US adult population) but there is non-response bias, then dps is
[bradley2021:L865] N
[bradley2021:L866] 3
[bradley2021:L867] = 10
[bradley2021:L868] 6
[bradley2021:L869] and the impact of ddc is amplified by the square root of that number (
[bradley2021:L870] \(\sqrt{{10}^{6}}=\mathrm{1,000}\)
[bradley2021:L871] ). By contrast, Axios–Ipsos reports a response rate of about
[bradley2021:L872] \(50 \% \)
[bradley2021:L873] , and obtains a sample of
[bradley2021:L874] n
[bradley2021:L875] = 1,000, so the dps could be as small as
[bradley2021:L876] N
[bradley2021:L877] 3
[bradley2021:L878] = 2,000 (with
[bradley2021:L879] \(\sqrt{\mathrm{2,000}}\approx 45\)
[bradley2021:L880] ).
[bradley2021:L881] This decomposition is why our comparison of the surveys is consistent with the ‘Law of Large Populations’
[bradley2021:L882] 1
[bradley2021:L883] (estimation error increases with
[bradley2021:L884] \(\sqrt{N}\)
[bradley2021:L885] ), even though all three surveys ultimately target the same US adult population. Given our existing knowledge about online–offline populations
[bradley2021:L886] 40
[bradley2021:L887] and our analysis of Axios–Ipsos’ small ‘offline’ population, Census Household Pulse may suffer from unrepresentativeness at Stage 1 of Extended Data Table
[bradley2021:L888] 3
[bradley2021:L889] , where
[bradley2021:L890] N
[bradley2021:L891] = 255 million, and Delphi–Facebook may suffer from unrepresentativeness at the initial stage of starting from the Facebook user base. By contrast, the main source of unrepresentativeness for Axios–Ipsos may be at a later stage at which the relevant population size is orders of magnitude smaller.
[bradley2021:L892] CDC estimates of vaccination rates
[bradley2021:L893] Our analysis of the nationwide vaccination rate covers the period between 9 January 2021 and 19 May 2021. We used CDC’s vaccination statistics published on their data tracker as of 26 May 2021. This dataset is a time series of counts of 1st dose vaccinations for every day in our time period, reported for all ages and disaggregated by age group.
[bradley2021:L894] This CDC time series obtained on 26 May 2021 included retroactive updates to dates covering our entire study period, as does each daily update provided by the CDC daily update. For example, the CDC benchmark we use for March 2021 is not only the vaccination counts originally reported in March but also includes the delayed reporting for March that the CDC became aware of by 26 May 2021. Analyzing several snapshots before 26 May 2021, we find that these retroactive updates 40 days out could change the initial estimate by about 5% (Extended Data Fig. 3), hence informing our sensitivity analysis of +/− 5% and 10% benchmark imprecision.
[bradley2021:L895] To match the sampling frame of the surveys we analyze, US adults 18 years and older, we must restrict the CDC vaccination counts to those administered to those adults. However, because of the different way states and jurisdiction report their vaccination statistics, the CDC did not possess age-coded counts for some jurisdictions, such as Texas, at the time of our study. The number of vaccinations with missing age data reached about 10 percent of the total US vaccinations at its peak at the time of our study. We therefore assume that the day by day fraction of adults among individuals for whom age is reported as missing is equal to the fraction of adults among individuals with age reported. Because minors became eligible for vaccinations only towards the end of our study period, the fraction of adults in data reporting age never falls below 97%.
[bradley2021:L896] Additional survey methodology
[bradley2021:L897] The Census Household Pulse and Delphi–Facebook surveys are the first of their kind for each organization, whereas Ipsos has maintained their online panel for 12 years.
[bradley2021:L898] Question wording
[bradley2021:L899] All three surveys ask whether respondents have received a COVID-19 vaccine (Extended Data Table
[bradley2021:L900] 1
[bradley2021:L901] ). Delphi–Facebook and Census Household Pulse ask similar questions (“Have you had/received a COVID-19 vaccination/vaccine?”). Axios–Ipsos asks “Do you personally know anyone who has already received the COVID-19 vaccine?”, and respondents are given response options including “Yes, I have received the vaccine.” The Axios–Ipsos question wording might pressure respondents to conform to their communities’ modal behaviour and thus misreport their true vaccination status, or may induce acquiescence bias from the multiple ‘yes’ options presented
[bradley2021:L902] 60
[bradley2021:L903] . This pressure may exist both in high- and low-vaccination communities, so its net effect on Axios–Ipsos’ results is unclear. Nonetheless, Axios–Ipsos’ question wording does differ from that of the other two surveys, and may contribute to the observed differences in estimates of vaccine uptake across surveys.
[bradley2021:L904] Population of interest
[bradley2021:L905] All three surveys target the US adult population, but with different sampling and weighting schemes. Census Household Pulse sets the denominator of their percentages as the household civilian, non-institutionalized population in the United States of 18 years of age or older, excluding Puerto Rico or the island areas. Axios–Ipsos designs samples to be representative of the US general adult population of 18 or older. For Delphi–Facebook, the US target population reported in weekly contingency tables is the US adult population, excluding Puerto Rico and other US territories. For the CDC Benchmark, we define the denominator as the US 18+ population, excluding Puerto Rico and other US territories. To estimate the size of the total US population, we use the US Census Bureau Annual Estimates of the Resident Population for the United States and Puerto Rico, 2019
[bradley2021:L906] 55
[bradley2021:L907] . This is also what the CDC uses as the denominator in calculating rates and percentages of the US population
[bradley2021:L908] 60
[bradley2021:L909] .
[bradley2021:L910] Axios–Ipsos and Delphi–Facebook generate target distributions of the US adult population using the Current Population Survey (CPS), March Supplement, from 2019 and 2018, respectively. Census Household Pulse uses a combination of 2018 1-year American Community Survey (ACS) estimates and the Census Bureau’s Population Estimates Program (PEP) from July 2020. Both the CPS and ACS are well-established large surveys by the Census and the choice between them is largely inconsequential.
[bradley2021:L911] Axios–Ipsos data
[bradley2021:L912] The Axios–Ipsos Coronavirus tracker is an ongoing, bi-weekly tracker intended to measure attitudes towards COVID-19 of adults in the US. The tracker has been running since 13 March 2020 and has released results from 45 waves as of 28 May 2021. Each wave generally runs over a period of 4 days. The Axios–Ipsos data used in this analysis were scraped from the topline PDF reports released on the Ipsos website
[bradley2021:L913] 5
[bradley2021:L914] . The PDF reports also contain Ipsos’ design effects, which we have confirmed are calculated as 1 plus the variance of the (scaled) weights.
[bradley2021:L915] Census Household Pulse data
[bradley2021:L916] The Census Household Pulse is an experimental product of the US Census Bureau in collaboration with eleven other federal statistical agencies. We use the point estimates presented in Data Tables, as well as the standard errors calculated by the Census Bureau using replicate weights. The design effects are not reported, however we can calculate it as
[bradley2021:L917] \(1+{{\rm{CV}}}_{{\rm{w}}}^{2}\)
[bradley2021:L918] , where CV
[bradley2021:L919] w
[bradley2021:L920] is the coefficient of variation of the individual-level weights included in the microdata
[bradley2021:L921] 23
[bradley2021:L922] .
[bradley2021:L923] Delphi–Facebook COVID symptom survey
[bradley2021:L924] The Delphi–Facebook COVID symptom survey is an ongoing survey collaboration between Facebook, the Delphi Group at Carnegie Mellon University (CMU), and the University of Maryland
[bradley2021:L925] 2
[bradley2021:L926] . The survey is intended to track COVID-like symptoms over time in the US and in over 200 countries. We use only the US data in this analysis. The study recruits respondents using daily stratified random samples recruiting a cross-section of Facebook active users. New respondents are obtained each day, and aggregates are reported publicly on weekly and monthly frequencies. The Delphi–Facebook data used here were downloaded directly from CMU’s repository for weekly contingency tables with point estimates and standard errors.
[bradley2021:L927] Ethical compliance
[bradley2021:L928] According to HRA decision tools (
[bradley2021:L929] http://www.hra-decisiontools.org.uk/research/
[bradley2021:L930] ), our study is considered Research, and according to the NHS REC review tool (
[bradley2021:L931] http://www.hra-decisiontools.org.uk/ethics/
[bradley2021:L932] ), we do not need NHS Research Ethics Committee (REC) review, as we used only (1) publicly available, (2) anonymized and (3) aggregated data outside of clinical settings.
[bradley2021:L933] Reporting summary
[bradley2021:L934] Further information on research design is available in the
[bradley2021:L935] Nature Research Reporting Summary
[bradley2021:L936] linked to this paper.
[bradley2021:L937] Data availability
[bradley2021:L938] Raw data have been deposited in the Harvard Dataverse, at
[bradley2021:L939] https://doi.org/10.7910/DVN/GKBUUK
[bradley2021:L940] . Data were collected from publicly available repositories of survey data by downloading the data directly or using APIs.
[bradley2021:L941] Code availability
[bradley2021:L942] Code to replicate the findings is available in the repository
[bradley2021:L943] https://github.com/vcbradley/ddc-vaccine-US
[bradley2021:L944] . The main decomposition of the ddc is available on the package ‘ddi’ from the Comprehensive R Archive Network (CRAN).

[barrero2023:L4] ooking back to 1965, full days worked from home were less than half 
[barrero2023:L6] a percent of all paid workdays in the United States. As shown in Figure 1, 
[barrero2023:L8] the work-from-home share rose slowly over the next few decades. In the 
[barrero2023:L10] 1970s, work from home often meant briefcases stuffed with paperwork. By the 
[barrero2023:L12] 1990s, it meant phone calls and floppy disks. In the twenty-first century, the rise of 
[barrero2023:L14] the internet facilitated collaboration at a distance. By 2019, the work-from-home 
[barrero2023:L16] share had reached 7 percent, and it seemed reasonable to anticipate a gradual rise 
[barrero2023:L18] in the years ahead.
[barrero2023:L20] Then came the pandemic. Social-distancing mandates and fear of COVID-19 
[barrero2023:L21] drove a sudden, massive shift to work from home. Much of that shift has endured. 
[barrero2023:L22] According to data from the US Census Bureau’s Household Pulse Survey (2022–
[barrero2023:L23] 2023) and our own Survey of Working Arrangements and Attitudes (Barrero et al. 
[barrero2023:L24] 2020–2023; https://wfhresearch.com/), full days worked from home account for 
[barrero2023:L25] 28 percent of paid workdays in June 2023—four times the estimated share for 2019.
[barrero2023:L26] The pandemic catalyzed the big shift to work from home, but earlier devel-
[barrero2023:L27] opments made it possible. Critical tools for remote work include web-based 
[barrero2023:L28] The Evolution of Work from Home

[barrero2023:L98] Work from Home over Time in the United States
[barrero2023:L99] Source: The figure reports estimates for full days worked from home as a percent of all paid workdays 
[barrero2023:L100] for persons 20–64 years of age, drawing on the American Heritage Time Use Study (AHTUS) 
[barrero2023:L101] (Fisher et al. 2018) for 1965, 1975, 1985, 1993, 1995 and 1998; the American Time Use Survey (ATUS) 
[barrero2023:L102] (Flood et al. 2023) from 2003 to 2019 by year; the Survey of Working Arrangements and Attitudes 
[barrero2023:L103] (SWAA) (Barrero et al. 2020–2023) for May 2020 and from July 2020 to June 2023 by month; and the 
[barrero2023:L104] Census Household Pulse Survey (HPS) (US Census Bureau 2022–2023) from May 2022 to June 2023 by 
[barrero2023:L105] month.  
[barrero2023:L106] Note: We use regression methods to adjust the SWAA data for question design changes. When using 
[barrero2023:L107] SWAA data, we restrict to persons who meet a prior-year earnings requirement of $20,000 in the 
[barrero2023:L108] March 2021 and prior survey waves, and $10,000 in the later waves. We weight the individual-level 
[barrero2023:L109] SWAA data to match the distribution across age-sex-education-earnings cells in the Current Population 
[barrero2023:L110] Survey. When using HPS data, we restrict to persons with household income greater than $25,000 and 
[barrero2023:L111] use the provided population weights. When using the ATUS, we restrict to persons with annual earnings 
[barrero2023:L112] of more than $20,000 in 2019 dollars calculated as 52 times weekly earnings deflated by the GDP deflator 
[barrero2023:L113] for Personal Consumption Expenditures (PCE) (US Bureau of Economic Analysis 2023). In the ATUS, 
[barrero2023:L114] we count paid workdays as ones in which the individual devotes six or more hours of work to their main 
[barrero2023:L115] job, regardless of work location, and we count work-from-home days as ones in which the individual works 
[barrero2023:L116] six or more hours on their main job at home. This approach corresponds to the SWAA-based measure. 
[barrero2023:L117] We use ATUS sample weights and drop the roughly 2 percent of observations flagged as “low quality.” 
[barrero2023:L118] We follow the same approach in the AHTUS as in the ATUS, except an absence of data prevents us from 
[barrero2023:L119] imposing the earnings requirement in 1965, 1993, 1995 and 1998. We use AHTUS sample weights and 
[barrero2023:L120] drop the 2–4 percent of observations flagged as low quality.
[barrero2023:L123] PAGINA PDF 3
[barrero2023:L124] José María Barrero, Nicholas Bloom, and Steven J. Davis      25
[barrero2023:L125] tools were ready for adoption and use at scale. Some of these tools function poorly 
[barrero2023:L126] without high-speed broadband. As it turns out, the share of American adults with 
[barrero2023:L127] broadband service at home rose from zero in 2000 to more than 70 percent in 
[barrero2023:L128] 2018 (Pew Research Center 2021). Thus, the infrastructure that supports home use 
[barrero2023:L129] of remote-collaboration tools also developed greatly before the pandemic struck. 
[barrero2023:L130] These developments created a foundation for the possibility of a big shift to work 
[barrero2023:L131] from home. 
[barrero2023:L132] A different issue is why the shift persisted after distancing mandates ended and 
[barrero2023:L133] after COVID death rates receded.1 Why have working arrangements not reverted 
[barrero2023:L134] to the prepandemic status quo? To turn the question around: If the big shift was 
[barrero2023:L135] possible (and apparently has been highly valued by many since it happened), why 
[barrero2023:L136] did it not happen sooner and more gradually? 
[barrero2023:L137] In considering an explanation, start with the obvious: The pandemic triggered 
[barrero2023:L138] a mass social experiment in working arrangements. Now consider two hypotheses. 
[barrero2023:L139] First, all of that experimentation generated a flood of new information, altering 
[barrero2023:L140] perceptions about the practicality and effectiveness of work from home. The simul-
[barrero2023:L141] taneity of experimentation across suppliers, producers, customers, and commercial 
[barrero2023:L142] networks also yielded information and experience that were hard or impossible to 
[barrero2023:L143] acquire before the pandemic. Second, employers and workers reoptimized over 
[barrero2023:L144] working arrangements in light of the new information. In particular, those with 
[barrero2023:L145] favorable experiences in the work-from-home mode opted to stick with higher 
[barrero2023:L146] work-from-home rates after the pandemic than before it struck.
[barrero2023:L147] To assess these two hypotheses, we surveyed tens of thousands of workers across 
[barrero2023:L148] many countries. In Barrero, Bloom, and Davis (2021b), we find that most American 
[barrero2023:L149] workers were favorably surprised by their ability to work from home productively 
[barrero2023:L150] during the pandemic. Similarly, in a sample of 27 countries, Aksoy et al. (2022) find 
[barrero2023:L151] that the average worker was favorably surprised by his or her ability to work from 
[barrero2023:L152] home during the pandemic. Both studies also find that the number of work-from-
[barrero2023:L153] home days that employers plan after the pandemic ends rise strongly with employee 
[barrero2023:L154] assessments of work-from-home productivity surprises. In short, many workers were 
[barrero2023:L155] favorably surprised by their ability to work remotely, and those favorable surprises 
[barrero2023:L156] led to a large, lasting shift to work from home.
[barrero2023:L157] The pandemic also spurred other developments that helped drive a lasting 
[barrero2023:L158] shift to work from home: new investments at home and inside organizations to 
[barrero2023:L159] facilitate remote work and improve its performance, learning-by-doing in the work-
[barrero2023:L160] from-home mode, pandemic-induced advances in products and technologies that 
[barrero2023:L161] support remote work, much greater social acceptance of work from home, and 
[barrero2023:L162] lingering concerns about infection risks that lead some people to prefer remote 
[barrero2023:L163] 1 COVID-19 deaths fluctuated sharply from February 2020 through March 2022 in the United States, 
[barrero2023:L164] before settling down to comparatively low levels. Weekly deaths per million reached nearly 47 in April 
[barrero2023:L165] 2020, peaked at 70 in January 2021, again rose above 50 in February 2022, and fell sharply in March 2022. 
[barrero2023:L166] Since then, the weekly death rate is less than 10 per million. See “Weekly confirmed COVID-19 deaths per 
[barrero2023:L167] million people” for the United States at https://ourworldindata.org/covid-deaths (accessed August 4, 
[barrero2023:L168] 2023).
[barrero2023:L171] PAGINA PDF 4
[barrero2023:L172] 26     Journal of Economic Perspectives
[barrero2023:L173] work. Aksoy et al. (2022) review the evidence for these developments. They also 
[barrero2023:L174] find higher levels of work from home as of mid-2021 and early 2022—and higher 
[barrero2023:L175] planned levels of work from home after the pandemic ends—in countries and 
[barrero2023:L176] regions with longer, stricter lockdown mandates during the pandemic. Thus, the 
[barrero2023:L177] full story of how the pandemic led to a large, lasting shift to work from home has 
[barrero2023:L178] multiple, reinforcing elements.
[barrero2023:L179] How the Big Shift Is Playing Out 
[barrero2023:L181] Newfound Variety in Working Arrangements
[barrero2023:L183] Before the pandemic, most workers had few options when it came to the 
[barrero2023:L184] remote-work intensity of attractive jobs. After the pandemic, choices in this regard 
[barrero2023:L185] exploded, especially for the college-educated. Work from home is now common 
[barrero2023:L186] in many jobs once seen as unsuited to remote work. The flip side of this expanded 
[barrero2023:L187] choice set for workers is a new set of opportunities and challenges for employers. 
[barrero2023:L188] Some organizations have embraced remote work as a means to improve recruitment 
[barrero2023:L189] and retention, moderate pay growth, reduce space needs, and cut overhead costs. 
[barrero2023:L190] Others have resisted remote work, because they see it as detrimental to productivity, 
[barrero2023:L191] innovation, and skills development or antithetical to their workplace cultures. 
[barrero2023:L192] As of 2023, 59 percent of full-time American employees (20–64 years of age) 
[barrero2023:L193] commute to their employer’s worksite or to a client’s location each workday. While 
[barrero2023:L194] most employees have resumed a traditional working arrangement, many have not. 
[barrero2023:L195] Twelve percent now work remotely on all or almost all workdays. Nearly 29 percent 
[barrero2023:L196] have a hybrid arrangement, whereby they split the workweek between home and 
[barrero2023:L197] their employer’s worksite. Traditional working arrangements continue to prevail in 
[barrero2023:L198] front-line retail jobs, restaurants and bars, hotels, transportation, construction jobs, 
[barrero2023:L199] manufactures, many healthcare jobs, janitorial and cleaning services, onsite security 
[barrero2023:L200] services, and other jobs that require face-to-face contact with customers, clients, and 
[barrero2023:L201] coworkers or the use of specialized equipment and dedicated facilities. Even amidst 
[barrero2023:L202] social-distancing mandates in 2020, people in these types of jobs typically worked at 
[barrero2023:L203] their employer’s site or not at all. 
[barrero2023:L204] As these remarks suggest, task requirements and production technologies 
[barrero2023:L205] sharply limit the possibilities for remote work in some jobs. Often, however, a partic-
[barrero2023:L206] ular job involves a bundle of tasks that differ in their suitability for remote work. In 
[barrero2023:L207] some jobs, like phone-based customer support, the bundle mainly involves tasks that 
[barrero2023:L208] can be performed effectively in an office setting, a home setting, or a mix of the two. 
[barrero2023:L209] In other jobs, like college professors, the bundle includes some tasks that require 
[barrero2023:L210] presence at the employer’s worksite (classroom teaching) and other tasks that do 
[barrero2023:L211] not (preparing lectures, grading assignments). More generally, the tasks associated 
[barrero2023:L212] with a given job fall along a spectrum from less-  to more-suitable for remote work. 
[barrero2023:L213] Unless the tasks for a given job are highly concentrated at the less-suitable 
[barrero2023:L214] end of the spectrum, work-from-home intensity reflects choices about job design, 
[barrero2023:L215] management practices, organizational culture, and life styles. These choices 
[barrero2023:L218] PAGINA PDF 5
[barrero2023:L219] The Evolution of Work from Home     27
[barrero2023:L220] respond to shifts in perceptions about the productivity of work from home, any 
[barrero2023:L221] stigma associated with the practice, the quality of tools that facilitate remote work, 
[barrero2023:L222] and an organization’s capacity to manage remote work. They also respond to shifts 
[barrero2023:L223] in employee desires to work from home, which is perhaps another legacy of the 
[barrero2023:L224] pandemic. When the tasks for a given job cluster near the more-suitable end of 
[barrero2023:L225] the spectrum, even modest shifts in worker preferences, productivity perceptions, 
[barrero2023:L226] stigma, tools for remote collaboration, and manager skill sets can yield big changes 
[barrero2023:L227] in the extent of work from home.2 
[barrero2023:L228] For employees with a hybrid arrangement, a typical pattern is to commute 
[barrero2023:L229] into the office on Tuesday, Wednesday, and Thursday and to focus those work-
[barrero2023:L230] days on meetings, presentations, training, mentoring, lunches with colleagues 
[barrero2023:L231] and clients, and other in-person tasks. Work from home on Mondays and Fridays 
[barrero2023:L232] can then focus on tasks that require individual effort and intense focus. Hybrid 
[barrero2023:L233] arrangements are common in many professional jobs, especially in middle and 
[barrero2023:L234] senior management positions. They have spread to the healthcare sector, where 
[barrero2023:L235] counseling services, prescription renewals, and routine consultations are now 
[barrero2023:L236] sometimes provided remotely, practices that were rare before the pandemic. Fully 
[barrero2023:L237] remote employees tend to work in information technology support functions, 
[barrero2023:L238] payroll processing, phone-based customer support and sales, and routine admin-
[barrero2023:L239] istrative functions that require few in-person interactions. Many fully remote jobs 
[barrero2023:L240] are computer-intensive, and most involve limited team work. Typically, the labor 
[barrero2023:L241] inputs in these jobs can be easily monitored, or the individual’s work product can 
[barrero2023:L242] be easily assessed. 
[barrero2023:L243] Table 1 provides information about how working arrangements differ by 
[barrero2023:L244] employer type in the US economy. While 59 percent of full-time employees work 
[barrero2023:L245] onsite each workday, only 33 percent of contractors and gig workers do so, and 
[barrero2023:L246] only 25 percent of other self-employed persons do so. Fully remote work is four 
[barrero2023:L247] times as common for the self-employed as for employees. Given these facts, people 
[barrero2023:L248] with strong desires to work in a fully remote capacity are more likely to choose 
[barrero2023:L249] self-employment, including contract and gig work. Regulations that clamp down 
[barrero2023:L250] on contract and gig work curtail choices and, in doing so, are likely to deter some 
[barrero2023:L251] people from working altogether. 
[barrero2023:L252] Working arrangements differ by employer size as well. In firms with fewer than 
[barrero2023:L253] 50 employees, two-thirds travel to their employer’s site each workday. In firms with 
[barrero2023:L254] 500 to 4,999 employees, the corresponding share is only 51 percent. Hybrid arrange-
[barrero2023:L255] ments are most common in firms with 500 to 4,999 employees, and fully remote jobs 
[barrero2023:L256] are most common in firms with 5,000 or more. Among government employees, the 
[barrero2023:L257] distribution of working arrangements is quite similar to that of all employees. 
[barrero2023:L258] 2 Dingel and Neiman (2020) exploit data on the nature of work and work activities to identify occupa-
[barrero2023:L259] tions that can be performed entirely from home. They estimate that 37 percent of American workers 
[barrero2023:L260] held such jobs before the pandemic. In many other jobs, some tasks are suitable for remote work. Thus, 
[barrero2023:L261] their evidence suggests that work-from-home intensity is a choice outcome in half or more of all US jobs.
[barrero2023:L264] PAGINA PDF 6
[barrero2023:L265] 28     Journal of Economic Perspectives
[barrero2023:L266] Work-from-Home Intensity by Industry
[barrero2023:L268] Figure 2 reports work-from-home rates by industry as of 2023. The Infor-
[barrero2023:L269] mation sector has the highest work-from-home rate at 2.6 days per week among 
[barrero2023:L270] employees who work at least five days a week. AirBnB, Upwork, and Yelp are well-
[barrero2023:L271] known examples of firms in the Information sector that operate with largely remote 
[barrero2023:L272] workforces. Meta (formerly Facebook), Shopify, and Twitter were among the first 
[barrero2023:L273] prominent companies to commit to high levels of remote work after the pandemic 
[barrero2023:L274] struck. They are also in the Information sector. Finance and Insurance and Profes-
[barrero2023:L275] sional and Business Services have the next highest work-from-home rates.
[barrero2023:L276] These three sectors share certain characteristics that facilitate or incentivize 
[barrero2023:L277] work from home: staff are well-paid, jobs are often analytical or computer-intensive 
[barrero2023:L278] in nature, and firms tend to cluster in major cities. Higher earners typically have 
[barrero2023:L279] nicer homes with more room for a home office. They also face higher marginal tax 
[barrero2023:L280] rates, intensifying the tax incentive to work from home. To see this point, suppose 
[barrero2023:L281] your boss offers the following choice: work at the office five days a week and get 
[barrero2023:L282] a 6 percent raise, or split the workweek between home and office for a 2 percent 
[barrero2023:L283] raise. Your cost of the hybrid option is 3 percent of after-tax pay at a 25 percent 
[barrero2023:L284] marginal tax rate but only 2 percent at a 50 percent marginal tax rate. Turning to
