[barrero2023:L370] another point, many computer-intensive jobs lend themselves to remote work. And 
[barrero2023:L371] many analytically oriented jobs require periods of intense focus, which is easier to 
[barrero2023:L372] find at home for many workers. Finally, because firms in these sectors cluster in 
[barrero2023:L373] dense urban centers, many of their employees face long commutes to the office. 
[barrero2023:L374] That makes it all the more appealing to avoid the commute, thereby saving time, 
[barrero2023:L375] money, and aggravation. 
[barrero2023:L376] At the other end of the industry distribution, employees in Retail, Hospitality, 
[barrero2023:L377] Food Services, Transportation, and Manufacturing have low work-from-home 
[barrero2023:L378] rates, ranging from 0.7 to 0.9 days per week. Most jobs in these industries require a 
[barrero2023:L379] physical presence to engage consumers or to work with specialized equipment and 
[barrero2023:L380] dedicated facilities. Because staff work mainly onsite, so do their managers.

[barrero2023:L383] granular data on millions of job vacancy postings. In one of their examples, they 
[barrero2023:L384] consider US automobile manufacturers recruiting for engineers. In 2022, and 
[barrero2023:L385] restricting attention to engineering positions, the share of postings that say the

[barrero2023:L437] 30     Journal of Economic Perspectives
[barrero2023:L438] job allows some remote work was zero for Tesla, 8 percent for Ford, 23 percent for 
[barrero2023:L439] General Motors, and 45 percent for Honda. It was near zero for all four companies 
[barrero2023:L440] in 2019. This example illustrates two of our key messages. First, work-from-home 
[barrero2023:L441] intensity is an outcome of choices about job design, managerial practices, and 
[barrero2023:L442] workplace culture. Organizations can make different choices in this regard, and 
[barrero2023:L443] they do so in the postpandemic economy. Second, the variety of working arrange-
[barrero2023:L444] ments on offer has exploded in the wake of the pandemic. 
[barrero2023:L445] Work from Home and Population Density
[barrero2023:L447] Our observations about how and why the work-from-home rate varies by 
[barrero2023:L448] industry suggest that it also varies systematically with local population density. That 
[barrero2023:L449] turns out to be the case, and powerfully so. To develop this point, we first assign 
[barrero2023:L450] zip codes to individual workers based on where they live (home location) or where 
[barrero2023:L451] their employer locates (job location). Next, we use data on residential population to 
[barrero2023:L452] sort zip codes (and workers) into population density bins. Finally, we compute full 
[barrero2023:L453] work-from-home days as a percent of all paid workdays in each bin.
[barrero2023:L454] Figure 3 displays the results, using blue dots when sorting by home location 
[barrero2023:L455] and red dots when sorting by job location. The same pattern emerges either way. 
[barrero2023:L456] The figure also highlights selected zip codes. For example, zip code 84720 falls into 
[barrero2023:L457] the second-lowest density bin and is one of two zip codes for Cedar City, Utah. Zip 
[barrero2023:L458] code 11225 falls into the top density bin and is one of more than 100 zip codes for 
[barrero2023:L459] Brooklyn, New York. The most sparsely populated zip codes, accounting for one-
[barrero2023:L460] fifth of US population, are to the left of the dashed line. 
[barrero2023:L461] In these sparsely populated areas of the United States, the average work-from-
[barrero2023:L462] home rate is 25 percent and varies little with population density. In the rest of the 
[barrero2023:L463] country, the work-from-home rate rises strongly with population density. In the top 
[barrero2023:L464] density bin, for example, work-from-home days account for more than 40 percent 
[barrero2023:L465] of all paid workdays. Industry mix, occupation mix, and local workforce demo-
[barrero2023:L466] graphics (age, sex, education) account for half of the density-related differences 
[barrero2023:L467] in work-from-home rates highlighted by Figure 3 (Buckman et al. 2023). Longer 
[barrero2023:L468] commutes also encourage high work-from-home rates in dense urban areas. 
[barrero2023:L469] High-quality internet service, which is more readily and widely available in urban 
[barrero2023:L470] settings, facilitates work from home and raises its productivity (Barrero, Bloom, 
[barrero2023:L471] and Davis 2021a). 
[barrero2023:L472] Other types of data tell a consistent story. As of July 2022, foot traffic in 
[barrero2023:L473] central business districts had recovered to nearly prepandemic levels in cities with 
[barrero2023:L474] fewer than 150,000 employees, but to only 60 percent of those levels in cities with 
[barrero2023:L475] 1.5 million or more employees (Monte, Porcher, and Rossi-Hansberg 2023). As of 
[barrero2023:L476] December 2022, the residential price premium for living near downtown returned 
[barrero2023:L477] to prepandemic levels in small cities, but remained considerably smaller than before 
[barrero2023:L478] the pandemic in large cities (Monte, Porcher, and Rossi-Hansberg 2023). From 
[barrero2023:L479] February 2020 to November 2022, Zillow’s US home price index rose 40 percent in 
[barrero2023:L480] exurban areas, 32 percent in suburban areas, 18 percent in high-density areas, and 
[barrero2023:L481] only 9 percent in city centers (Ramani and Bloom 2022). As of May 2023, US public 
[barrero2023:L484] PAGINA PDF 9
[barrero2023:L485] The Evolution of Work from Home     31
[barrero2023:L486] transit ridership was only 70 percent of its May 2019 level.3 All of these statistics 
[barrero2023:L487] confirm that the big shift to work from home is concentrated in high-density areas. 
[barrero2023:L488] Work from Home and Demographic Characteristics 
[barrero2023:L490] Education. Work-from-home intensity rises steeply with education in the 
[barrero2023:L491] postpandemic economy. Among Americans 20–64 years of age with a high school 
[barrero2023:L492] education or less, full days worked from home account for 20 percent of all paid 
[barrero2023:L493] workdays. The corresponding figure is 26 percent for those with some college, 
[barrero2023:L494] 34 percent for those with a four-year college degree, and 36 percent for persons 
[barrero2023:L495] 3 See US Bureau of Transportation Statistics, Public Ridership Transit, retrieved from FRED, Federal 
[barrero2023:L496] Reserve Bank of St. Louis, https://fred.stlouisfed.org/series/TRANSIT, accessed on August 29, 2023.

[barrero2023:L547] with a graduate degree.4 As one might anticipate from this pattern, work-from-
[barrero2023:L548] home intensity also rises steeply with earnings (Barrero, Bloom, and Davis 2021b).
[barrero2023:L549] In line with our remarks about jobs as task bundles and the evidence in Dingel 
[barrero2023:L550] and Neiman (2020), occupation and industry are strong predictors of whether 
[barrero2023:L551] someone works from home. Highly educated workers are concentrated in the 
[barrero2023:L552] industry sectors of Information, Finance & Insurance, and Professional & Business 
[barrero2023:L553] Services. These sectors—and the people who work in them—have high work-from-
[barrero2023:L554] home intensities for the reasons discussed above.
[barrero2023:L555] Age. Figure 4 shows how work-from-home intensity varies by age in the United 
[barrero2023:L556] States. It is lowest among people in their early 20s and peaks among those in 
[barrero2023:L557] their 30s. People in their 20s have high returns to professional networking, 
[barrero2023:L558] on-the-job training, and mentoring—activities that benefit greatly from in-person 
[barrero2023:L559] interactions. Young workers may also place more value on socializing at the 
[barrero2023:L560] workplace or nearby. They are more likely to live in small or shared apartments, 
[barrero2023:L561] which reduces the appeal of work from home. People in their 30s and early 40s 
[barrero2023:L562] are more likely to live with children and face long commutes, raising the appeal of 
[barrero2023:L563] 4 These statistics reflect data from the January–June 2023 waves of the Survey of Working Arrangements 
[barrero2023:L564] and Attitudes (Barrero et al. 2020–2023).

[barrero2023:L589] José María Barrero, Nicholas Bloom, and Steven J. Davis      33
[barrero2023:L590] work from home. Older employees may be less keen to work from home because 
[barrero2023:L591] they no longer have childcare responsibilities, or simply because they like to 
[barrero2023:L592] socialize at the workplace.
[barrero2023:L593] In unreported results, we investigate the role of children and marital status in 
[barrero2023:L594] the life-cycle pattern of Figure 4. Consider the raw gap of 5.7 percentage points in 
[barrero2023:L595] work-from-home intensity between workers who are 30–34 years old and those who 
[barrero2023:L596] are 20–24. When we use regression methods to control for the presence of children 
[barrero2023:L597] under 14 in the household, this gap shrinks by one percentage point. Controlling 
[barrero2023:L598] for marital status has no effect on the gap, and marital status itself is statistically 
[barrero2023:L599] insignificant. Similarly, controlling for the presence of children shrinks the raw gap 
[barrero2023:L600] of 4.8 points between workers who are 30–34 and those who are 60–64 by about 
[barrero2023:L601] 1.6 points, with no impact of marital status. In short, the timing of children over the 
[barrero2023:L602] life cycle partly explains the pattern in Figure 4.
[barrero2023:L603] Sex. Figure 5 shows, perhaps surprisingly, that American women work from 
[barrero2023:L604] home at only modestly greater rates than men. In the first half of 2023, full days 
[barrero2023:L605] worked from home are 29.3 percent of paid workdays among women, 20–64 years 
[barrero2023:L606] old, as compared to 27.0 percent among men. This gap is statistically significant, 
[barrero2023:L607] but small compared to the gap between college and noncollege workers and to the 
[barrero2023:L608] changes over time. Higher levels of education among women push up their work-
[barrero2023:L609] from-home rates, other things equal. When we use regression methods to control 
[barrero2023:L610] for educational attainment, the gap between women and men shrinks to 1.3 points. 
[barrero2023:L611] When we also include industry and occupation fixed effects in the regression 
[barrero2023:L612] specification, the gap between men and women is 1.5 percentage points. In other 
[barrero2023:L613] research, Le Barbanchon, Rathelot, and Roulet (2021) find that women place more 
[barrero2023:L614] value than men on reductions in commuting time. That aspect of their preferences 
[barrero2023:L615] pushes in the direction of higher work-from-home intensity for women relative to 
[barrero2023:L616] otherwise similar men.
[barrero2023:L617] Parents. People who live with children value the ability to work from home more 
[barrero2023:L618] highly. In a cross-section of advanced and middle-income countries, workers living 
[barrero2023:L619] with children under 14 express a greater willingness-to-pay for the option to work 
[barrero2023:L620] from home two or three days a week, as compared to observationally similar workers 
[barrero2023:L621] without children (Aksoy et al. 2022). The effect holds for men and women and is 
[barrero2023:L622] pervasive across countries.
[barrero2023:L623] Moving from preferences to outcomes, we find that people with children do 
[barrero2023:L624] indeed work from home at higher rates, as foreshadowed by our discussion of 
[barrero2023:L625] Figure 4. Table 2 develops this point more fully, again drawing on data from the 
[barrero2023:L626] Survey of Working Arrangements and Attitudes (Barrero et al. 2020–2023). All 
[barrero2023:L627] specifications control for five-year age bins and month fixed effects. The coeffi-
[barrero2023:L628] cient on “Female” in column 2 says that full days worked from home (as a percent 
[barrero2023:L629] of all paid workdays) are 1.0 percentage points higher for women than men, the 
[barrero2023:L630] omitted group. The other coefficient in column 2 says that full days worked from 
[barrero2023:L631] home are 4.5 percentage points higher for workers who live with children under 
[barrero2023:L632] 14. Column 3 adds a term to capture the interaction between “Female” and “Chil-
[barrero2023:L633] dren under 14.” The –2.3 coefficient on this term is statistically significant at the 
[barrero2023:L636] PAGINA PDF 12
[barrero2023:L637] 34     Journal of Economic Perspectives
[barrero2023:L638] 5 percent level. In other words, living with children is associated with a larger 
[barrero2023:L639] marginal increase in work-from-home intensity for men than women. The coef-
[barrero2023:L640] ficient on the main effect for “Children under 14” is now 5.5 percentage points. 
[barrero2023:L641] Finally, when we add controls for the worker’s education, industry, and occu-
[barrero2023:L642] pation in columns 4 and 5, we continue to find higher work-from-home rates 
[barrero2023:L643] among those who live with children. The results in columns 4 and 5 also confirm 
[barrero2023:L644] that education is a powerful predictor of work-from-home intensity. (Here, the 
[barrero2023:L645] omitted group is persons with no postsecondary education.) However, the coef-
[barrero2023:L646] ficients on the main and interaction effects for women are no longer statistically 
[barrero2023:L647] significant.

[barrero2023:L699] The Evolution of Work from Home     35
[barrero2023:L700] Let us summarize the demographic patterns. First, work-from-home intensity 
[barrero2023:L701] rises strongly with the education of individual workers. This pattern is highly robust 
[barrero2023:L702] to controls for other demographic characteristics, marital status, and the presence 
[barrero2023:L703] of children at home. The presence of children is also robustly associated with more 
[barrero2023:L704] work from home. Women work from home slightly more than men, but this already-
[barrero2023:L705] small difference shrinks further or disappears altogether when controlling for 
[barrero2023:L706] women’s greater education. Finally, the small R-squared values in the Table 2 regres-
[barrero2023:L707] sions warrant attention, because they imply that many other factors also influence 
[barrero2023:L708] who works from home and how much.
[barrero2023:L709] Other Countries
[barrero2023:L711] The extent of work from home differs greatly across countries, although 
[barrero2023:L712] it is hard to say exactly how much. One useful source of information is Google

[barrero2023:L790] 36     Journal of Economic Perspectives
[barrero2023:L791] ­Workplace Mobility, which, until October 2022, tracked the frequency of work-
[barrero2023:L792] place visits by country and month. As of October 2022, workplace visits in the 
[barrero2023:L793] United States were down about 25 percentage points relative to the January 
[barrero2023:L794] 2020 benchmark. That drop roughly matches the corresponding jump (relative 
[barrero2023:L795] to 2019) in work-from-home days shown in Figure 1. Canada and the United 
[barrero2023:L796] Kingdom show similar-sized drops in workplace visits (again, as of October 2022, 
[barrero2023:L797] and relative to the prepandemic benchmark). A selection of European countries 
[barrero2023:L798] (France, Germany, Italy, Spain, the Netherlands, Sweden, and Portugal) shows a 
[barrero2023:L799] smaller drop of 21 percentage points. Elsewhere, the drops are smaller yet. For 
[barrero2023:L800] example, workplace mobility is down only nine points in Australia as of October 
[barrero2023:L801] 2022, and by even less in Japan, South Korea, Singapore, and Taiwan.
[barrero2023:L802] Along with coauthors in Aksoy et al. (2022, 2023b), we have developed 
[barrero2023:L803] another source of data on work-from-home intensity that covers many countries: 
[barrero2023:L804] The Global Survey of Working Arrangements (https://wfhresearch.com/gswa-
[barrero2023:L805] data), which samples full-time employees who have at least a secondary education. 
[barrero2023:L806] According to these data, full-time employees worked from home 1.4 days a week 
[barrero2023:L807] in the United States as of March-April 2023, more than any other country except 
[barrero2023:L808] Canada (1.7 days) and the United Kingdom (1.5 days). By way of comparison, the 
[barrero2023:L809] average across 15 countries in continental Europe is only 0.8 days a week, with a 
[barrero2023:L810] maximum of 1.0 days in Germany and The Netherlands. The average across six 
[barrero2023:L811] Asian countries (China, Malaysia, Japan, Singapore, South Korea, and Taiwan) is 
[barrero2023:L812] 0.7 days a week.
[barrero2023:L813] Why does the United States have a higher work-from-home intensity than most 
[barrero2023:L814] other countries? We think several factors are at work. First, residences tend to be 
[barrero2023:L815] larger in the United States than in Europe and Asia, making it easier to accom-
[barrero2023:L816] modate a home office. Second, the United States has an unusually high share of 
[barrero2023:L817] workers in Information, Finance and Insurance, and Professional and Business 
[barrero2023:L818] Services. As discussed earlier, these sectors share several characteristics that facilitate 
[barrero2023:L819] or encourage work from home. Third, managerial practices around performance 
[barrero2023:L820] measurement and evaluation tend to be more advanced at US firms than at firms in 
[barrero2023:L821] other countries (for example, Scur et al. 2021). Strong practices in this regard are 
[barrero2023:L822] especially beneficial when managers (and coworkers) cannot observe employees 
[barrero2023:L823] directly as they perform their work-related activities. 
[barrero2023:L824] Finally, most countries in Asia and many in Europe were more successful 
[barrero2023:L825] than the United States in mitigating COVID-related deaths and hospitalizations. 
[barrero2023:L826] And they often did so without tight, extended clampdowns on commercial activi-
[barrero2023:L827] ties. South Korea and Taiwan are especially striking examples in this regard. 
[barrero2023:L828] Because of their less dire COVID experiences—and, in many cases, their more 
[barrero2023:L829] limited clampdowns on commercial activity—people and organizations in other 
[barrero2023:L830] countries experimented less with remote work, made fewer investments in systems 
[barrero2023:L831] and management practices that support remote work, had less learning-by-doing 
[barrero2023:L832] in work-from-home mode, and had smaller shifts in attitudes and norms around 
[barrero2023:L833] remote work. In short, several factors that reinforced the big shift to work from 
[barrero2023:L834] home in the United States acted with less force in most other countries.
[barrero2023:L837] PAGINA PDF 15
[barrero2023:L838] José María Barrero, Nicholas Bloom, and Steven J. Davis      37
[barrero2023:L839] What the Big Shift Means for Pay, Productivity, and Innovation 
[barrero2023:L841] Labor Costs and the Wage Structure
[barrero2023:L843] The rise of remote work affects labor costs and wages in several ways. For one 
[barrero2023:L844] thing, it can have important effects on productivity, which feeds into labor costs and 
[barrero2023:L845] wages. For the moment, and for the sake of analytical clarity, let us consider how 
[barrero2023:L846] remote work affects labor costs and wages net of any productivity effects. The rise 
[barrero2023:L847] of remote work makes it easier for firms situated in high-wage areas to recruit and 
[barrero2023:L848] employ staff in areas with lower wages. There is also evidence that quit rates and 
[barrero2023:L849] turnover costs fall when a firm lets its employees adopt hybrid working arrange-
[barrero2023:L850] ments (Bloom, Han, and Liang 2023). And standard economic models imply that 
[barrero2023:L851] the rise of remote work puts downward pressure on real wages through labor supply 
[barrero2023:L852] effects. 
[barrero2023:L853] To see this last point, start by observing that most people prefer to work 
[barrero2023:L854] remotely part of the week. On average, Americans value the option to work from 
[barrero2023:L855] home two or three days a week at 8 percent of pay, according to data from the 
[barrero2023:L856] Survey of Working Arrangements and Attitudes (Barrero et al. 2020–2023). The 
[barrero2023:L857] experimental study by Mas and Pallais (2017) finds similar estimates for a narrower 
[barrero2023:L858] group of workers. In the textbook model of a competitive labor market, a new-
[barrero2023:L859] found job amenity raises the supply of labor at any given wage. In addition, when 
[barrero2023:L860] employees work from home, they devote part of their commute-time savings to their 
[barrero2023:L861] jobs (Aksoy et al. 2023a). That also adds to labor supply. Finally, the big shift creates 
[barrero2023:L862] new job opportunities for people who live in remote locations and economically 
[barrero2023:L863] depressed areas, couples facing joint-location constraints, parents who want to be 
[barrero2023:L864] near their children while working, people with mobility impairments that make it 
[barrero2023:L865] hard to commute, and those who find it hard to cope with face-to-face interactions 
[barrero2023:L866] in the workplace. New job opportunities afforded by work from home will draw 
[barrero2023:L867] some of these people into the labor force, expanding labor supply. For any given 
[barrero2023:L868] labor demand curve, outward shifts in labor supply reduce the equilibrium real 
[barrero2023:L869] wage. In an inflationary environment, this real wage drop can come about through 
[barrero2023:L870] slower nominal wage growth.
[barrero2023:L871] Bargaining models also imply that the rise of remote work puts downward 
[barrero2023:L872] pressure on wages. In this class of models, the employer and employee each get a 
[barrero2023:L873] share of the value generated by the employment relationship. Initially, employees 
[barrero2023:L874] captured the full amenity value of the big shift to work from home, because wages 
[barrero2023:L875] reflected bargains made before the surprise onset of the pandemic. Over time, as 
[barrero2023:L876] compensation packages adjust, employers capture a portion of the amenity-value 
[barrero2023:L877] gains associated with the rise of remote work. Employer benefits take the form of 
[barrero2023:L878] slower wage growth during the transition to a new equilibrium with pay packages 
[barrero2023:L879] that reflect higher remote work levels. 
[barrero2023:L880] Has the rise of remote work actually exerted a material influence on wages 
[barrero2023:L881] and other labor costs? If so, how much? Thus far, these questions have attracted 
[barrero2023:L882] scant attention from researchers. In Barrero et al. (2022), we provide some initial 
[barrero2023:L883] evidence. We surveyed more than 600 business executives at US firms in April and 
[barrero2023:L886] PAGINA PDF 16
[barrero2023:L887] 38     Journal of Economic Perspectives
[barrero2023:L888] May 2022 as part of the Survey of Business Uncertainty. We asked each executive 
[barrero2023:L889] the following question: “Over the past twelve months, has your firm expanded the 
[barrero2023:L890] opportunities to work from home (or other remote locations) as a way to keep 
[barrero2023:L891] employees happy and to moderate wage-growth pressures?” If the executive says 
[barrero2023:L892] “Yes”—and about four in ten do so—we then ask, “What is your best estimate for 
[barrero2023:L893] how much expanded remote-work opportunities have moderated wage-growth 
[barrero2023:L894] pressures at your firm in the past twelve months?” We also ask analogous questions 
[barrero2023:L895] about work-from-home plans in the next twelve months. When we aggregate over all 
[barrero2023:L896] responses to the look-back and look-ahead questions, we obtain a cumulative wage-
[barrero2023:L897] growth moderation of 2.0 percentage points over two years, centered on spring 
[barrero2023:L898] 2022. In computing this average, we include the majority of executives who say work 
[barrero2023:L899] from home has not impacted their firm’s wages.
[barrero2023:L900] An effect of 2.0 percentage points is a material restraint on nominal wage 
[barrero2023:L901] growth. It came during a critical period for monetary policy, as the Fed sought to 
[barrero2023:L902] curtail the strong inflationary pressures that emerged in the latter part of 2021 and 
[barrero2023:L903] 2022. The same survey also uncovers evidence that the rise in remote work is associ-
[barrero2023:L904] ated with more reliance on part-time employees (at 26 percent of firms), more use 
[barrero2023:L905] of independent contractors (23 percent), more use of leased workers (11 percent), 
[barrero2023:L906] more offshoring of jobs (8 percent), and more employment of physically-challenged 
[barrero2023:L907] persons. These developments also tend to reduce labor costs. 
[barrero2023:L908] The big shift to work from home is likely to alter the structure of wages as well. 
[barrero2023:L909] To see how, recall that the shift is concentrated among college-educated workers 
[barrero2023:L910] and in certain industry sectors (as shown in Figure 2). The amenity-value bene-
[barrero2023:L911] fits associated with the big shift are concentrated among these same workers and 
[barrero2023:L912] sectors. A long line of thinking in economics says that wages are lower, other things 
[barrero2023:L913] equal, in jobs with amenity attributes that workers like.5 Thus, we hypothesize that 
[barrero2023:L914] the big shift shrank the college wage premium and put more downward pressure on 
[barrero2023:L915] wages in sectors with larger increases in work-from-home intensity. 
[barrero2023:L916] While the available evidence is limited, it favors this hypothesis. In their survey 
[barrero2023:L917] of business executives, Barrero et al. (2022) find smaller wage-growth moderation 
[barrero2023:L918] effects in sectors with few jobs that are suitable for remote work and larger moder-
[barrero2023:L919] ation effects in sectors with many such jobs. Autor, Dube, and McGrew (2023) 
[barrero2023:L920] document a large and “unexpected compression” in the US wage distribution after 
[barrero2023:L921] the pandemic struck, including a reduction in the college wage premium. Their 
[barrero2023:L922] explanation stresses the pandemic’s effects on labor market tightness and wage 
[barrero2023:L923] markdowns, but they observe that amenity-value shocks may also play a role.
[barrero2023:L924] There is room for much more research into how the big shift affects labor costs 
[barrero2023:L925] and the wage structure. In addition to the channels we have stressed, work from 
[barrero2023:L926] home has the potential to influence profoundly the extent of spatial competition 
[barrero2023:L927] in labor markets, including monopsony power in local labor markets. Especially in 
[barrero2023:L928] 5 Rosen (1986) offers a classic statement of this theory of “equalizing differences” or “compensating 
[barrero2023:L929] differentials.” As he notes, the basic idea originates in The Wealth of Nations by Adam Smith.  For a recent 
[barrero2023:L930] discussion of compensating differentials in this journal, see Lavetti (2023).
[barrero2023:L933] PAGINA PDF 17
[barrero2023:L934] The Evolution of Work from Home     39
[barrero2023:L935] fully remote jobs, competition from workers in other countries can exert a powerful 
[barrero2023:L936] influence on wages. For evidence on this theme using data from a web-based job 
[barrero2023:L937] platform with global reach, see Brinatti et al. (2022).
[barrero2023:L938] Productivity Effects
[barrero2023:L940] The productivity implications of the big shift to work from home have sparked 
[barrero2023:L941] vigorous debate among business leaders, researchers, and pundits. Managers differ 
[barrero2023:L942] greatly in their views about how remote work affects productivity in their own orga-
[barrero2023:L943] nizations (Bloom et al. 2023a). Worker perceptions also differ greatly in this regard 
[barrero2023:L944] (Barrero, Bloom, and Davis 2021b).
[barrero2023:L945] The controversies and disagreements surrounding the productivity effects of 
[barrero2023:L946] work from home reflect the complex nature of the issue. Jobs and tasks differ greatly 
[barrero2023:L947] in their suitability for remote work, as do workers, managerial styles, and workplace 
[barrero2023:L948] cultures. Thus, there is no sound reason to expect the productivity effects of remote 
[barrero2023:L949] work to be uniform across jobs, workers, managers, and organizations. In addition, 
[barrero2023:L950] communications, performance evaluations, and management practices must adapt 
[barrero2023:L951] to new working arrangements, if they are to work well. Adaptation often requires 
[barrero2023:L952] new skills, perhaps especially for managers. The adaptation process itself involves a 
[barrero2023:L953] good deal of trial and error and learning-by-doing. Thus, the productivity effects of 
[barrero2023:L954] work from home are likely to unfold over months or years. 
[barrero2023:L955] With these cautions in mind, we now offer several remarks on how produc-
[barrero2023:L956] tivity relates to working arrangements. For us, the chief question of interest is the 
[barrero2023:L957] following: How will a large, lasting shift to work from home affect productivity 
[barrero2023:L958] outcomes when working arrangements are a matter of choice, rather than necessity, 
[barrero2023:L959] and when organizations and individuals have adapted to their preferred working 
[barrero2023:L960] arrangements? Previous studies differ in whether, and how clearly, they throw light 
[barrero2023:L961] on the answer to this question.
[barrero2023:L962] Productivity concepts. From a manager’s perspective, it makes sense to think of 
[barrero2023:L963] labor productivity as work output per paid time unit. This labor productivity concept 
[barrero2023:L964] corresponds to what statistical agencies typically seek to measure. For employees, in 
[barrero2023:L965] contrast, it makes sense to count commuting time as part of the total time devoted 
[barrero2023:L966] to their jobs. This approach also makes sense from a societal perspective, because 
[barrero2023:L967] commuting time is part of the resources expended in producing market output. 
[barrero2023:L968] This conceptual distinction matters. Recall from Figure 1 that full days worked 
[barrero2023:L969] from home rose by 21 percentage points from 2019 to June 2023. That corresponds 
[barrero2023:L970] to 1.05 fewer commuting days per week. When working from home (rather than 
[barrero2023:L971] on employer premises), the average daily savings in commuting and grooming 
[barrero2023:L972] time is 65 minutes for American workers. So, the big shift generates time savings of 
[barrero2023:L973] 68 minutes per week per worker, which is about 2.8 percent of a 40-hour workweek. 
[barrero2023:L974] Standard productivity measures published by statistical agencies miss this source of 
[barrero2023:L975] productivity gains associated with the big shift.6 
[barrero2023:L976] 6 A fuller analysis of the missing productivity gain would account for three other factors. First, the big shift 
[barrero2023:L977] is concentrated among workers with higher earnings, who also tend to have longer commutes. Adjusting 
[barrero2023:L980] PAGINA PDF 18
[barrero2023:L981] 40     Journal of Economic Perspectives
[barrero2023:L982] Productivity perceptions. On balance, workers hold more favorable views about 
[barrero2023:L983] productivity in work-from-home mode than managers. Much of this difference 
[barrero2023:L984] in perceptions turns on the same conceptual distinction. Consider someone who 
[barrero2023:L985] works eight paid hours a day, lives 30 minutes from the office, and accomplishes 
[barrero2023:L986] the same amount whether working from home or the office. In this example, total 
[barrero2023:L987] time devoted to work is nine hours per day when commuting, and eight hours 
[barrero2023:L988] per day when working from home. So, the worker perceives (correctly) that he 
[barrero2023:L989] or she accomplishes the same amount in 11 percent less time when working from 
[barrero2023:L990] home—a big productivity boost! From the manager’s vantage point, however, the 
[barrero2023:L991] productivity effect of work from home is nil in this example. Thus, managers and 
[barrero2023:L992] workers can quite reasonably hold different views about the productivity effects of 
[barrero2023:L993] work from home.
[barrero2023:L994] According to data from the Survey of Working Arrangements and Attitudes 
[barrero2023:L995] covering January to June 2023, 43 percent of workers able to work from home say 
[barrero2023:L996] they are more productive when working from home, and 14 percent say they are 
[barrero2023:L997] less productive. The rest say their productivity is about the same either way. Probing 
[barrero2023:L998] further, workers attribute the self-assessed productivity benefits of work from home 
[barrero2023:L999] mainly to the savings in commute time (Barrero, Bloom, and Davis 2021b). When 
[barrero2023:L1000] we net out the productivity benefit that workers attribute to commute time-savings, 
[barrero2023:L1001] the manager-worker gap in perceptions about the productivity effects of work from 
[barrero2023:L1002] home shrinks to a small difference.
[barrero2023:L1003] There is a broader lesson here: Disagreements about the productivity effects 
[barrero2023:L1004] of work from home turn partly on what counts as productivity. Workers regard 
[barrero2023:L1005] commute time-savings as a source of productivity gains, while managers do not. 
[barrero2023:L1006] Workers may be blind to the managerial challenges of remote work. Managers, 
[barrero2023:L1007] especially senior ones, are probably more concerned about its implications for 
[barrero2023:L1008] workplace culture. Remote work may let seasoned employees accomplish more in 
[barrero2023:L1009] the near term while, at the same time, detracting from the transmission of their 
[barrero2023:L1010] knowledge to younger coworkers—with detrimental effects on the organization’s 
[barrero2023:L1011] productivity over the longer term. 
[barrero2023:L1012] Fully Remote Work. Several studies find that fully remote work yields lower 
[barrero2023:L1013] productivity than on-site work. Emanuel and Harrington (2023) analyze data from 
[barrero2023:L1014] a Fortune 500 firm that, before the pandemic, operated call centers with both 
[barrero2023:L1015] remote and on-site employees in the same jobs. In response to the pandemic, 
[barrero2023:L1016] the firm shifted all employees in these jobs to fully remote work. Productivity 
[barrero2023:L1017] among formerly onsite employees fell 4 percent relative to that of already-remote 
[barrero2023:L1018] employees. Emanuel and Harrington also find evidence that the closure of phys-
[barrero2023:L1019] ical call centers reduced call quality, especially among less experienced employees. 
[barrero2023:L1020] for these patterns would raise the 2.8 percent figure, especially on an earnings-weighted basis. Second, 
[barrero2023:L1021] workers devote between 30 and 40 percent of their commute time-savings to their jobs. Adjusting for 
[barrero2023:L1022] that would lower the 2.8 percent figure. Third, hours worked are observed with error in the data that 
[barrero2023:L1023] statistical agencies use to measure labor productivity, and the observational errors may be correlated with 
[barrero2023:L1024] the big shift itself. See Davis (2022) for additional discussion. 
[barrero2023:L1027] PAGINA PDF 19
[barrero2023:L1028] José María Barrero, Nicholas Bloom, and Steven J. Davis      41
[barrero2023:L1029] These findings are noteworthy, in part, because they involve a firm with prior 
[barrero2023:L1030] experience in managing fully remote call-center workers. Presumably then, 
[barrero2023:L1031] the firm had already adapted its systems and practices to manage fully remote 
[barrero2023:L1032] workers.
[barrero2023:L1033] Gibbs, Mengel, and Siemroth (2023) study productivity outcomes for skilled 
[barrero2023:L1034] professionals at a large Indian technology services company. In March 2020, the 
[barrero2023:L1035] company abruptly shifted all employees to fully remote work in response to the 
[barrero2023:L1036] pandemic. Immediately after the shift, average worktime rose by 1.5 hours per day 
[barrero2023:L1037] and output fell slightly according to their primary performance measure. They esti-
[barrero2023:L1038] mate that the shift to remote work lowered average labor productivity (output per 
[barrero2023:L1039] hour worked) by 8 to 19 percent. They also provide evidence that greater commu-
[barrero2023:L1040] nication and coordination costs drove much of the measured productivity drop. In 
[barrero2023:L1041] particular, time spent on meetings and coordination activities rose, crowding out 
[barrero2023:L1042] time devoted to a concentrated focus on work tasks.
[barrero2023:L1043] The productivity concept matters here, as well. Table 2 of Gibbs, Mengel, and 
[barrero2023:L1044] Siemroth (2023) reports an average two-way commute time of 1.3 hours per day. 
[barrero2023:L1045] That is, the savings in commute time largely offsets the extra 1.5 hours per day that 
[barrero2023:L1046] employees put into their jobs after the transition to fully remote work. From the 
[barrero2023:L1047] worker’s perspective, and from a societal perspective as well, the company’s shift to 
[barrero2023:L1048] remote work had small effects on productivity. The larger point is that the commute 
[barrero2023:L1049] time-savings from remote work can offset sizable drops in productivity, as conven-
[barrero2023:L1050] tionally measured.
[barrero2023:L1051] Gibbs, Mengel, and Siemroth (2023) also find that employees “narrowed the 
[barrero2023:L1052] scope of their networks” after shifting to fully remote work, engaging in fewer contacts 
[barrero2023:L1053] inside and outside the organization. Other studies also find scope-narrowing and 
[barrero2023:L1054] siloing effects associated with remote work. For example, Yang et al. (2022) find that 
[barrero2023:L1055] communications among 61,000 Microsoft employees became more asynchronous 
[barrero2023:L1056] after a pandemic-induced shift to remote work, and collaborations became more 
[barrero2023:L1057] static and siloed. Battiston, Blanes i Vidal, and Kirchmaier (2021) find that police 
[barrero2023:L1058] dispatchers work faster, especially in busy periods, when they sit in the same room 
[barrero2023:L1059] as other dispatchers. These three studies suggest, in various ways, that remote work 
[barrero2023:L1060] can slow communications, impede the diffusion of knowledge within an organiza-
[barrero2023:L1061] tion, and narrow the scope of collaborative efforts. 
[barrero2023:L1062] Many managers and workers also express concerns about mentoring and 
[barrero2023:L1063] learning in remote settings. According to a survey by the Pew Research Center, 
[barrero2023:L1064] 36 percent of American employees who work from home all, most, or some of the 
[barrero2023:L1065] time believe that teleworking hurts their opportunities to be mentored at work 
[barrero2023:L1066] “a lot/a little” (Parker 2023). However, 10 percent think it helps. With respect to 
[barrero2023:L1067] “Chances of getting ahead in their job” and “Whether they are given important 
[barrero2023:L1068] assignments,” large majorities think that working from home neither helps nor 
[barrero2023:L1069] hurts, and the rest are evenly split between helps and hurts. One interpretation of 
[barrero2023:L1070] these results is that the impact of work from home on mentoring and job advance-
[barrero2023:L1071] ment depends on the person, the job, and the way the organization manages its 
[barrero2023:L1072] remote workers. 
[barrero2023:L1075] PAGINA PDF 20
[barrero2023:L1076] 42     Journal of Economic Perspectives
[barrero2023:L1077] In a study of mentoring practices and team relations, Emanuel, Harrington, 
[barrero2023:L1078] and Pallais (2023) consider software engineers at a large technology firm. Some 
[barrero2023:L1079] engineering teams were housed in the same building, and other teams were split 
[barrero2023:L1080] across two adjacent buildings. Before the pandemic, employees housed in the same 
[barrero2023:L1081] building as teammates received 21 percent more comments on their code from 
[barrero2023:L1082] coworkers. These comments provide suggestions on how to improve code, and they 
[barrero2023:L1083] play an important role in employee learning and performance gains. When the 
[barrero2023:L1084] pandemic struck, all employees shifted to work from home. The comment rate differ-
[barrero2023:L1085] ence between colocated and other teams vanished, and overall comment volume 
[barrero2023:L1086] fell by almost half. The volume drop was largest for junior employees, reinforcing 
[barrero2023:L1087] concerns that remote work impedes knowledge transmission to younger workers. 
[barrero2023:L1088] Other concerns about the productivity effects of remote work center on moti-
[barrero2023:L1089] vation and self-control. As one gag line has it, the three enemies of working from 
[barrero2023:L1090] home are the bed, the refrigerator, and the television. Indeed, college students 
[barrero2023:L1091] sometimes study in libraries as a commitment device—even though grades, 
[barrero2023:L1092] self-esteem, and future career prospects would seem to provide powerful incentives 
[barrero2023:L1093] already. So, it is perhaps unsurprising that some people struggle with motivation 
[barrero2023:L1094] when working in a remote capacity. Armed with a bit of self-knowledge, they may 
[barrero2023:L1095] also choose to work at their employer’s site despite the attractions of work from 
[barrero2023:L1096] home.
[barrero2023:L1097] A fuller analysis of why organizations employ fully remote workers, even when 
[barrero2023:L1098] it means lower labor productivity, would look to other cost savings. We discussed 
[barrero2023:L1099] how remote work can lower labor costs. In addition, fully remote employees do not 
[barrero2023:L1100] require office space and the overhead costs that come with a physical footprint. 
[barrero2023:L1101] These cost savings must be considered along with the productivity consequences, 
[barrero2023:L1102] now and in the future, when optimizing over working arrangements. At the orga-
[barrero2023:L1103] nizational level, this optimization problem is a complex one, because a shift in 
[barrero2023:L1104] working arrangements also requires new skills and work habits, new managerial 
[barrero2023:L1105] practices, and new organizational capabilities if remote work is to be effective. 
[barrero2023:L1106] Hybrid arrangements. Studies of hybrid working arrangements often find produc-
[barrero2023:L1107] tivity gains (relative to traditional arrangements) or no discernable effect. In an 
[barrero2023:L1108] early study, Bloom et al. (2015) consider a field experiment with 250 call-center 
[barrero2023:L1109] employees at cTrip.com, a large Chinese travel agent. Employees were randomly 
[barrero2023:L1110] allocated between two groups. One group continued working five days a week in the 
[barrero2023:L1111] office, and the other group switched to a hybrid arrangement with four home work-
[barrero2023:L1112] days and one office workday each week. Average daily output rose by 13 percent 
[barrero2023:L1113] among switchers. Nine percentage points of this output gain arose from extra work-
[barrero2023:L1114] time due to shorter breaks and less sick leave. The other four points reflect greater 
[barrero2023:L1115] efficiency in the form of more calls handled per work hour. 
[barrero2023:L1116] In another prepandemic experiment, Choudhury, Foroughi, and Larson (2021) 
[barrero2023:L1117] study the productivity of patent examiners employed by the US Patent and Trade-
[barrero2023:L1118] mark Office. Starting in 2006, several hundred examiners were allowed to shift to 
[barrero2023:L1119] a hybrid arrangement with up to four home workdays per week. The shift took 
[barrero2023:L1120] place in a staggered manner, with exogenous timing at the worker level, facilitating 
[barrero2023:L1123] PAGINA PDF 21
[barrero2023:L1124] The Evolution of Work from Home     43
[barrero2023:L1125] the estimation of causal effects. Patent actions rose by 5 percent, on average, after 
[barrero2023:L1126] examiners switched to the hybrid arrangement. The switch had no impact on the 
[barrero2023:L1127] incidence of rework, indicating that quality did not suffer. Later, starting in 2012, 
[barrero2023:L1128] patent examiners were allowed to live anywhere in the contiguous United States 
[barrero2023:L1129] with periodic travel to headquarters at their own expense. Patent actions rose by an 
[barrero2023:L1130] additional 8 percent among those who switched from the hybrid to the work-from-
[barrero2023:L1131] anywhere arrangement. Choudhury et al. provide evidence that these productivity 
[barrero2023:L1132] gains reflected greater effort by patent examiners.
[barrero2023:L1133] Three other aspects of the setting in this study warrant attention. First, patent 
[barrero2023:L1134] examinations are performed by individuals, not teams. Second, examiners were 
[barrero2023:L1135] required to spend at least two years working in the office before transitioning to 
[barrero2023:L1136] the hybrid arrangement—and more time in that arrangement before transitioning 
[barrero2023:L1137] to a work-from-anywhere arrangement. Third, individual examiners chose whether 
[barrero2023:L1138] to switch from a traditional working arrangement to the alternative arrangements. 
[barrero2023:L1139] Thus, the selection process into the alternative arrangements reflects individual 
[barrero2023:L1140] preferences and self-knowledge about ability to work remotely.
[barrero2023:L1141] Other recent studies analyze more modest shifts from traditional to hybrid 
[barrero2023:L1142] working arrangements. Choudhury et al. (2022) consider an organization in 
[barrero2023:L1143] Bangladesh that randomly assigned administrative staff to three groups: high 
[barrero2023:L1144] work-from-home intensity (more than 40 percent of workdays), intermediate 
[barrero2023:L1145] intensity (23-40 percent), and low intensity (0–23 percent). Staff in the inter-
[barrero2023:L1146] mediate group sent more emails to more people, drafted more complex emails, 
[barrero2023:L1147] reported better job satisfaction, felt more connected to colleagues, and received 
[barrero2023:L1148] stronger performance evaluations from managers. Bloom, Han, and Liang (2023) 
[barrero2023:L1149] conduct a randomized control trial with 1600 highly educated employees (software 
[barrero2023:L1150] engineers and marketing and finance professionals) at cTrip.com. Those born on 
[barrero2023:L1151] even-numbered dates continued to work onsite five days a week, while the rest had 
[barrero2023:L1152] the option to work from home on Wednesday and Friday. The experiment ran 
[barrero2023:L1153] for six months, yielding zero or small productivity gains from the hybrid arrange-
[barrero2023:L1154] ment. Employees highly valued the hybrid arrangement, except for managers. 
[barrero2023:L1155] Angelici and Profeta (2023) consider a nine-month experiment that injected flex-
[barrero2023:L1156] ibility into the working arrangements of full-time employees at a large Italian firm. 
[barrero2023:L1157] The control group stuck to a traditional arrangement that prescribes time and 
[barrero2023:L1158] place of work throughout the week. In the treated group, white-collar employees 
[barrero2023:L1159] chose where and when to work—and blue-collar employees chose when—one day 
[barrero2023:L1160] a week. Productivity rose 10 percent, on average, among the treated relative to the 
[barrero2023:L1161] controls, as measured by self-assessments and by supervisors. The treated group 
[barrero2023:L1162] also reported higher levels of well-being and work-life balance. Greater flexibility 
[barrero2023:L1163] for treated workers had no apparent effect on the productivity of coworkers on 
[barrero2023:L1164] the same team.
[barrero2023:L1165] All three of these studies suggest that working from home one or two days a 
[barrero2023:L1166] week improves productivity and leads to happier employees. This pattern supports 
[barrero2023:L1167] the view that, in many jobs, some tasks are well suited for remote work. The produc-
[barrero2023:L1168] tivity gains associated with hybrid working arrangements in these studies could 
[barrero2023:L1171] PAGINA PDF 22
[barrero2023:L1172] 44     Journal of Economic Perspectives
[barrero2023:L1173] reflect greater effort levels by happier employees, quieter work environments at 
[barrero2023:L1174] home, and the time savings that employees put back into their jobs.
[barrero2023:L1175] The Pace of Innovation
[barrero2023:L1177] Face-to-face interactions in the workplace can foster the diffusion of knowledge 
[barrero2023:L1178] and the generation of new ideas. Looking beyond a single workplace, cities have 
[barrero2023:L1179] long functioned as hubs for knowledge spillovers across workers, firms, and indus-
[barrero2023:L1180] tries and as centers of invention, innovation, and entrepreneurship.7 In this light, 
[barrero2023:L1181] the big shift to work from home raises concerns about its potential to slow the pace 
[barrero2023:L1182] of innovation and the growth rate of productivity.
[barrero2023:L1183] In one study that speaks to these concerns, Brucks and Levav (2022) designed 
[barrero2023:L1184] and fielded commercial innovation experiments in cooperation with employers in 
[barrero2023:L1185] Finland, Hungary, India, Israel, and Portugal. More than 800 engineering teams 
[barrero2023:L1186] were tasked with suggesting new uses for an existing product, with teams randomly 
[barrero2023:L1187] assigned to meet in person or via videoconference. In-person teams proved more 
[barrero2023:L1188] effective, in that their product ideas received higher external ratings. When it came 
[barrero2023:L1189] to selecting ideas (rather than developing them), teams performed as well, or even 
[barrero2023:L1190] better, when they met by videoconference. These results suggest that brainstorming 
[barrero2023:L1191] activities benefit from in-person meetings, but some other aspects of the innovation 
[barrero2023:L1192] process do not. In a similar spirit, Lin, Frey, and Wu (2023) develop evidence that 
[barrero2023:L1193] collaboration at a distance is especially challenging in the early stages of research 
[barrero2023:L1194] “when an idea is hard to articulate and knowledge is tacit.” They show that inventor 
[barrero2023:L1195] teams that collaborate remotely make fewer breakthroughs, as measured by patent 
[barrero2023:L1196] citations. The same pattern holds for scientific publications. Lin, Frey, and Wu 
[barrero2023:L1197] also show that collaboration in dispersed teams is more focused on technical tasks, 
[barrero2023:L1198] including data collection and analysis, rather than idea generation and research 
[barrero2023:L1199] design. Both studies suggest that in-person communications are particularly valu-
[barrero2023:L1200] able in the early stages of the innovation process but less so in later stages and in 
[barrero2023:L1201] technical tasks. 
[barrero2023:L1202] Profit-oriented firms have strong incentives to recognize and respond to the 
[barrero2023:L1203] internal costs and benefits of working arrangements. However, if those arrangements 
[barrero2023:L1204] yield idea spillovers beyond the boundaries of the firm, outcomes need not be effi-
[barrero2023:L1205] cient or socially desirable. Monte, Porcher, and Rossi-Hansberg (2023) pursue this 
[barrero2023:L1206] theme. In their calibrated model, a city can settle at a low or high work-from-home 
[barrero2023:L1207] intensity. The low-intensity equilibrium yields more spillovers, greater productivity, 
[barrero2023:L1208] and higher social welfare in their large-city calibration. Still, that equilibrium can be 
[barrero2023:L1209] disrupted by a shock that, for a period time, renders it infeasible or unattractive to 
[barrero2023:L1210] commute to the worksite. Even after the shock ends, workers and firms can settle at 
[barrero2023:L1211] a new equilibrium with less commuting (a benefit), lower productivity (a cost), and 
[barrero2023:L1212] lower social welfare. Their model offers an alternative explanation for the enduring 
[barrero2023:L1213] character of the big shift to work from home after the pandemic. Although Monte, 
[barrero2023:L1214] 7 The literature is vast and diverse. See Carlino and Kerr (2015) and Combes and Gobillon (2015) for 
[barrero2023:L1215] reviews.
[barrero2023:L1218] PAGINA PDF 23
[barrero2023:L1219] José María Barrero, Nicholas Bloom, and Steven J. Davis      45
[barrero2023:L1220] Porcher, and Rossi-Hansberg focus on how work-from-intensity affects the level of 
[barrero2023:L1221] productivity, a similar point applies to the pace of innovation. In other words, it is 
[barrero2023:L1222] possible that a shock-induced shift to work from home could slow the pace of inno-
[barrero2023:L1223] vation by undermining the idea-generating capabilities of cities.
[barrero2023:L1224] Despite these reasons for concern, we are optimistic about the outlook for 
[barrero2023:L1225] the pace of innovation, at least as it relates to working arrangements. The same 
[barrero2023:L1226] developments that facilitated a big shift to work from home also created greater 
[barrero2023:L1227] reach and higher quality in one-way and two-way communications at a distance. 
[barrero2023:L1228] These include the rise of the internet, better broadband infrastructure, better 
[barrero2023:L1229] videoconferencing, the emergence of the cloud, and better software tools for 
[barrero2023:L1230] remote collaboration. 
[barrero2023:L1231] Chen, Frey, and Presidente (2022) also study the relationship of remote collab-
[barrero2023:L1232] oration to the impact of scientific articles, as reflected in citations. Before 2010, 
[barrero2023:L1233] remote collaboration produced articles that were more incremental in nature 
[barrero2023:L1234] and less likely to yield “disruptive” advances, echoing the findings in Lin, Frey, 
[barrero2023:L1235] and Wu (2023). However, Chen, Frey, and Presidente also show that the quality 
[barrero2023:L1236] discount on articles written by dispersed teams shrinks over time, vanishes around 
[barrero2023:L1237] 2010, and then becomes a premium. A plausible explanation is that advances in 
[barrero2023:L1238] remote-collaboration technologies made it easier and cheaper to coordinate a 
[barrero2023:L1239] broader range of specialized and geographically scattered complementary inputs. 
[barrero2023:L1240] In the model of Becker and Murphy (1992, Section 6) such a fall in coordination 
[barrero2023:L1241] costs raises the innovation rate.
[barrero2023:L1242] In an earlier study, Forman and van Zeebroeck (2012) examine patenting activity 
[barrero2023:L1243] inside firms before and after the adoption of internet technologies, which happened 
[barrero2023:L1244] at different times in different firms. Adoption led to more patent-producing 
[barrero2023:L1245] collaborations by geographically dispersed teams within the firm. Evidently, internet 
[barrero2023:L1246] access lowered coordination costs among geographically dispersed scientists in the 
[barrero2023:L1247] same firm. In later work, Forman and van Zeebroeck (2019) find that the spread 
[barrero2023:L1248] of internet connectivity increased knowledge flows across locations, as reflected in 
[barrero2023:L1249] between-location patent citations within firms. 
[barrero2023:L1250] In conversations with work-from-home skeptics, we often hear some version of 
[barrero2023:L1251] the following claims: Working from home means a loss of serendipitous encounters 
[barrero2023:L1252] in and around the workplace. Or, knowledge flows more readily among people who 
[barrero2023:L1253] work in the same location. Another claim is that people generate more and better 
[barrero2023:L1254] ideas, and bring them to fruition more quickly, when they work in close proximity. 
[barrero2023:L1255] Even if these claims are true, they miss much. 
[barrero2023:L1256] First, there is an opportunity cost to chatting with your coworker in the next-door 
[barrero2023:L1257] office: You could be collaborating with your far-away coauthor via Zoom. It is hardly 
[barrero2023:L1258] obvious that serendipitous encounters in the workplace foster innovation better 
[barrero2023:L1259] than planned encounters selected from a much larger universe. Second, remote-
[barrero2023:L1260] collaboration technologies tremendously expand the opportunities to form teams 
[barrero2023:L1261] that are optimized for particular research projects and questions. This advantage 
[barrero2023:L1262] in team formation is especially valuable in projects that call for the cooperation of 
[barrero2023:L1263] many scientists with distinct skills and types of expertise. Third, if some aspects of the 
[barrero2023:L1266] PAGINA PDF 24
[barrero2023:L1267] 46     Journal of Economic Perspectives
[barrero2023:L1268] innovation process truly benefit from in-person communications, then researchers 
[barrero2023:L1269] have strong incentives to focus on those aspects when they are in the same location. 
[barrero2023:L1270] Hybrid working arrangements, in particular, do not prevent in-person meetings. 
[barrero2023:L1271] They only require more forethought and coordination to make them happen.
[barrero2023:L1272] Looking Ahead
[barrero2023:L1274] At the outset of this essay, we explained how the pandemic catalyzed a big shift 
[barrero2023:L1275] to work from home and why it will endure. Statistical evidence confirms this conclu-
[barrero2023:L1276] sion. According to Figure 1, the US work-from-home rate in 2023 has settled at about 
[barrero2023:L1277] four times the 2019 level. Office occupancy statistics from Kastle Security Systems 
[barrero2023:L1278] (n.d.) also indicate that work-from-home intensity has stabilized in 2023.
[barrero2023:L1279] As part of the July 2023 Survey of Business Uncertainty, fielded by the Atlanta 
[barrero2023:L1280] Federal Reserve, we asked US business executives about the work-from-home outlook 
[barrero2023:L1281] at their own firms. The survey responses cover about 500 firms distributed widely 
[barrero2023:L1282] across industries, states, and firm size categories. Specifically, we asked: “Looking 
[barrero2023:L1283] forward to five years from now, what share of your firm’s full-time employees do you 
[barrero2023:L1284] expect to be in each category [fully in person, hybrid, fully remote] in 2028?” We 
[barrero2023:L1285] asked a parallel question about the firm’s current working arrangements. Compiling 
[barrero2023:L1286] the results, executives anticipate modest increases over the next five years in both 
[barrero2023:L1287] the fully remote share and the hybrid share (Bloom et al. 2023b). This finding holds 
[barrero2023:L1288] whether we weight each firm equally or in proportion to its number of employees. 
[barrero2023:L1289] Another reason to anticipate steady or slowly rising work-from-home rates over 
[barrero2023:L1290] the next several years is that organizations will continue to adapt their practices to 
[barrero2023:L1291] manage hybrid and fully remote workers more effectively. That will raise produc-
[barrero2023:L1292] tivity in work-from-home mode. Where experience teaches that remote work is 
[barrero2023:L1293] unsuitable, organizations will revert to traditional arrangements, if they not have 
[barrero2023:L1294] done so already.
[barrero2023:L1295] Yet another reason involves the innovation incentives created by the big shift. 
[barrero2023:L1296] A growing market provides incentives for investments in innovations to serve that 
[barrero2023:L1297] market (Schmookler 1966). The US market for technologies and products that 
[barrero2023:L1298] support remote work is now four times as large as in 2019. It has also become much 
[barrero2023:L1299] larger in the rest of the world. To assess the force of the Schmookler effect, Bloom, 
[barrero2023:L1300] Davis, and Zhestkova (2021) consider the monthly flow of newly filed applications 
[barrero2023:L1301] for US patents. They use automated text readings to determine which ones claim 
[barrero2023:L1302] to advance technologies in support of video conferencing, telecommuting, remote 
[barrero2023:L1303] collaboration, and work from home. Patent applications that advance these tech-
[barrero2023:L1304] nologies double as a share of all newly filed US patent applications from January 
[barrero2023:L1305] to September 2020. In ongoing research with Mihai Codreanu, we find that this 
[barrero2023:L1306] redirection of innovation efforts has continued through at least early 2022. So, it 
[barrero2023:L1307] is reasonable to anticipate that remote-collaboration technologies and tools will 
[barrero2023:L1308] continue to advance at a rapid pace for some years to come, further reinforcing the 
[barrero2023:L1309] shift to remote work. 
[barrero2023:L1312] PAGINA PDF 25
[barrero2023:L1313] The Evolution of Work from Home     47
[barrero2023:L1314] We see the outlook for fully remote jobs as less secure than the one for hybrid 
[barrero2023:L1315] arrangements. Cost pressures encourage firms to shift fully remote jobs to coun-
[barrero2023:L1316] tries with lower wages, including countries with many English-proficient workers. Of 
[barrero2023:L1317] course, that process has been underway for decades. The new element is that firms 
[barrero2023:L1318] now have better information about which jobs and tasks are well suited for remote 
[barrero2023:L1319] work. The automation of call-center jobs and routine administrative tasks may also 
[barrero2023:L1320] reduce the number of fully remote jobs in the United States. 
[barrero2023:L1321] Work-from-home rates will continue to differ sharply across industries, occu-
[barrero2023:L1322] pations, cities, and worker education levels. One corollary is that an economic 
[barrero2023:L1323] downturn concentrated in remote-intensive sectors could lower the overall work-
[barrero2023:L1324] from-home rate for a time. Another corollary is that the amenity-value benefits of 
[barrero2023:L1325] the big shift will also remain unevenly distributed across sectors, occupations, and 
[barrero2023:L1326] demographic groups. As a result, we think the big shift will have lasting effects on 
[barrero2023:L1327] the structure of wages. It may also profoundly influence the nature and intensity of 
[barrero2023:L1328] competition in the labor market. 
[barrero2023:L1329] ■

[barrero2023:L1472] 50     Journal of Economic Perspectives


FUENTE bloom2024: Bloom, Han y Liang (2024), Nature 630, 920-925, DOI 10.1038/s41586-024-07500-2
URL: https://www.nature.com/articles/s41586-024-07500-2
[bloom2024:L53] Abstract
[bloom2024:L54] Working from home has become standard for employees with a university degree. The most common scheme, which has been adopted by around 100 million employees in Europe and North America, is a hybrid schedule, in which individuals spend a mix of days at home and at work each week
[bloom2024:L55] 1
[bloom2024:L56] ,
[bloom2024:L57] 2
[bloom2024:L58] . However, the effects of hybrid working on employees and firms have been debated, and some executives argue that it damages productivity, innovation and career development
[bloom2024:L59] 3
[bloom2024:L60] –
[bloom2024:L61] 5
[bloom2024:L62] . Here we ran a six-month randomized control trial investigating the effects of hybrid working from home on 1,612 employees in a Chinese technology company in 2021–2022. We found that hybrid working improved job satisfaction and reduced quit rates by one-third. The reduction in quit rates was significant for non-managers, female employees and those with long commutes. Null equivalence tests showed that hybrid working did not affect performance grades over the next two years of reviews. We found no evidence for a difference in promotions over the next two years overall, or for any major employee subgroup. Finally, null equivalence tests showed that hybrid working had no effect on the lines of code written by computer-engineer employees. We also found that the 395 managers in the experiment revised their surveyed views about the effect of hybrid working on productivity, from a perceived negative effect (−2.6% on average) before the experiment to a perceived positive one (+1.0%) after the experiment. These results indicate that a hybrid schedule with two days a week working from home does not damage performance.
[bloom2024:L63] Subject terms:
[bloom2024:L64] Economics, Research management, Business
[bloom2024:L65] A trial investigating the effects of a hybrid working schedule in a Chinese technology firm in 2021–2022 shows that working from home two days a week improved job satisfaction, reduced quitting and did not affect performance.
[bloom2024:L66] status
[bloom2024:L67] released
[bloom2024:L68] display-pdf
[bloom2024:L69] yes
[bloom2024:L70] is-in-collection-domain
[bloom2024:L71] yes
[bloom2024:L72] is-olf
[bloom2024:L73] no

[bloom2024:L75] no

[bloom2024:L77] no
[bloom2024:L78] is-journal-matter
[bloom2024:L79] no

[bloom2024:L81] no

[bloom2024:L83] no
[bloom2024:L84] Received 2023 Aug 15; Accepted 2024 Apr 30; Issue date 2024.
[bloom2024:L85] Main
[bloom2024:L86] Working from home (WFH) surged after the COVID-19 pandemic, with university-graduate employees typically WFH for one to two days a week during 2023 (refs.
[bloom2024:L87] 2
[bloom2024:L88] ,
[bloom2024:L89] 6
[bloom2024:L90] ). Previous causal research on WFH has focused on employees who are fully remote, usually working on independent tasks in call-centre, data-entry and helpdesk roles. This literature has found that the effects of fully remote working on productivity are often negative, which has resulted in calls to curtail WFH
[bloom2024:L91] 5
[bloom2024:L92] –
[bloom2024:L93] 12
[bloom2024:L94] . However, there are two challenges when it comes to interpreting this literature. First, more than 70% of employees WFH globally are on a hybrid schedule. This group comprises more than 100 million individuals, with the most common working pattern being three days a week in the office and two days a week at home
[bloom2024:L95] 2
[bloom2024:L96] ,
[bloom2024:L97] 8
[bloom2024:L98] ,
[bloom2024:L99] 9
[bloom2024:L100] . Second, most employees who are regularly WFH are university graduates in creative team jobs that are important in science, law, finance, information technology (IT) and other industries, rather than performing repetitive data-entry or call processing tasks
[bloom2024:L101] 10
[bloom2024:L102] ,
[bloom2024:L103] 11
[bloom2024:L104] .
[bloom2024:L105] This paper addresses the gap in previous studies in two key ways. First, it uses a randomized control trial to examine the causal effect of a hybrid schedule in which employees are allowed to WFH two days per week. Second, it focuses on university-graduate employees in software engineering, marketing, accounting and finance, whose activities are mainly creative team tasks.
[bloom2024:L106] Our study describes a randomized control trial from August 2021 to January 2022, which involved 1,612 graduate employees in the Airfare and IT divisions of a large Chinese travel technology multinational called Trip.com. Employees were randomized by even or odd birthdays into the option to WFH on Wednesday and Friday and come into the office on the other three days, or to come into the office on all five days.
[bloom2024:L107] We found that in the hybrid WFH (‘treatment’) group, attrition rates dropped by one-third (mean
[bloom2024:L108] control
[bloom2024:L109] = 7.20, mean
[bloom2024:L110] treat
[bloom2024:L111] = 4.80,
[bloom2024:L112] t
[bloom2024:L113] (1610) = 2.02,
[bloom2024:L114] P
[bloom2024:L115] = 0.043) and work satisfaction scores improved (mean
[bloom2024:L116] control
[bloom2024:L117] = 7.84, mean
[bloom2024:L118] treat
[bloom2024:L119] = 8.19,
[bloom2024:L120] t
[bloom2024:L121] (1343) = 4.17,
[bloom2024:L122] P
[bloom2024:L123] < 0.001). Employees reported that WFH saved on commuting time and costs and afforded them the flexibility to attend to occasional personal tasks during the day (and catch up in the evenings or weekends). These effects on reduced attrition were significant for non-managerial employees (mean
[bloom2024:L124] control
[bloom2024:L125] = 8.59, mean
[bloom2024:L126] treat
[bloom2024:L127] = 5.33,
[bloom2024:L128] t
[bloom2024:L129] (1215) = 2.23,
[bloom2024:L130] P
[bloom2024:L131] = 0.026), female employees (mean
[bloom2024:L132] control
[bloom2024:L133] = 9.19, mean
[bloom2024:L134] treat
[bloom2024:L135] = 4.18,
[bloom2024:L136] t
[bloom2024:L137] (568) = 2.40,
[bloom2024:L138] P
[bloom2024:L139] = 0.017) and those with long (above-median) commutes (mean
[bloom2024:L140] control
[bloom2024:L141] = 6.00, mean
[bloom2024:L142] treat
[bloom2024:L143] = 2.89,
[bloom2024:L144] t
[bloom2024:L145] (609) = 1.87,
[bloom2024:L146] P
[bloom2024:L147] = 0.062).
[bloom2024:L148] At the same time, we found no evidence of a significant effect on employees’ performance reviews, on the basis of null equivalence tests, and no evidence of a difference in promotion rates over periods of up to two years (‘Null results’ section of the
[bloom2024:L149] Methods
[bloom2024:L150] ). We did find significant differences in pre-experiment beliefs about the effects of WFH on productivity between non-managers and managers. Before the experiment, managers tended to have more negative views, reporting that hybrid WFH would be likely to affect productivity by −2.6%, whereas non-managers had more positive views (+0.7%) (
[bloom2024:L151] t
[bloom2024:L152] (1313) = −4.56,
[bloom2024:L153] P
[bloom2024:L154] < 0.001). After the experiment, the views of managers increased to +1.0%, converging towards non-managers’ views (mean
[bloom2024:L155] non-manager
[bloom2024:L156] = 1.62, mean
[bloom2024:L157] manager
[bloom2024:L158] = 1.05,
[bloom2024:L159] t
[bloom2024:L160] (1343) = −0.945,
[bloom2024:L161] P
[bloom2024:L162] = 0.345). This highlights how the experience of hybrid working leads to a more positive assessment of its effect on productivity—consistent with the overall experience in Asia, the Americas and Europe throughout the pandemic, where perceptions of WFH improved considerably
[bloom2024:L163] 13
[bloom2024:L164] .
[bloom2024:L165] The experiment
[bloom2024:L166] The experiment took place at Trip.com, the third-largest global travel agent by sales in 2019. Trip.com was established in 1999, was quoted on NASDAQ in 2003 and was worth about US$20 billion at the time of the experiment. It is headquartered in Shanghai, with offices across China and internationally, and has roughly 35,000 employees.
[bloom2024:L167] In the summer of 2021, Trip.com decided to evaluate the effects of hybrid WFH on the 1,612 engineering, marketing and finance employees in the Airfare and IT divisions, spanning 395 managers and 1,217 non-managers. All experimental participants were surveyed at baseline, with questions on expectations, background and their interest in volunteering for early participation in the experiment. The firm randomized employees with an odd-number birthday (born on the first, third, fifth and so on day of the month) into the treatment group.
[bloom2024:L168] Figure
[bloom2024:L169] 1
[bloom2024:L170] shows two pictures of employees working in the office to highlight three points. First, in the second half of 2021, COVID incidence rates in Shanghai were so low that employees were neither masked nor socially distanced at the office. Although the COVID pandemic had led to lockdowns in early 2020 and during 2022, during the second half of 2021, Shanghai employees were free to come to work, and typically were unmasked in the office. Second, employees worked in modern open-plan offices in desk groupings of four or six colleagues from the same team, reflecting the importance of collaboration. Third, the office is a large modern building, similar to many large Asian, European and North American offices.
[bloom2024:L171] Fig. 1
[bloom2024:L172] Trip.com employees worked in modern open-plan offices, with teams seated together.
[bloom2024:L173] Pictures of Trip.com employees in the office during the experiment. The people in the experimental sample are typically in their mid-30s, and 65% are male. All of them have a university undergraduate degree and 32% have a postgraduate degree, usually in computer science, accounting or finance, at the master’s or PhD level. They have 6.4 years tenure on average and 48% of employees have children (Extended Data Table
[bloom2024:L174] 1
[bloom2024:L175] ).
[bloom2024:L176] Effects on employee retention
[bloom2024:L177] One key motivation for Trip.com in running the experiment was to evaluate how hybrid WFH affected employee attrition and job satisfaction. The net effect was to reduce attrition over the experiment by 2.4%, which against the control-group base of 7.2% was a one-third (33%) reduction in attrition (mean
[bloom2024:L178] control
[bloom2024:L179] = 7.20, mean
[bloom2024:L180] treat
[bloom2024:L181] = 4.80,
[bloom2024:L182] t
[bloom2024:L183] (1610) = 2.02,
[bloom2024:L184] P
[bloom2024:L185] = 0.043). Consistent with this reduction in quit rates, employees in the treatment group also registered more positive responses to job-satisfaction surveys (mean
[bloom2024:L186] control
[bloom2024:L187] = 7.84, mean
[bloom2024:L188] treat
[bloom2024:L189] = 8.19,
[bloom2024:L190] t
[bloom2024:L191] (1343) = 4.17,
[bloom2024:L192] P
[bloom2024:L193] < 0.001). Employees were anonymously surveyed on 21 January 2022, and employees in the treatment group showed significantly higher scores on a scale from 0 (lowest) to 10 (highest) in ‘work–life balance’, ‘work satisfaction’, ‘life satisfaction’ and ‘recommendation to friends’, and significantly lower scores in ‘intention to quit’ (Extended Data Table
[bloom2024:L194] 2
[bloom2024:L195] ).
[bloom2024:L196] Extended Data Table 2
[bloom2024:L197] Job-satisfaction-survey measures were higher for employees in the treatment group
[bloom2024:L198] Sample from 1345 employees (446 volunteers, 899 non-volunteers) in the end-line survey. Values for intention to quit range from −0.325 (lowest) to 0.325 (highest). Values for the other variables range from 0 (lowest) to 10 (highest). For example, ‘Would recommend to friends’ ranges from ‘Definitely no’ at 0 to ‘Definitely yes’ at 10.
[bloom2024:L199] P
[bloom2024:L200] values are calculated using two-tailed
[bloom2024:L201] t
[bloom2024:L202] -tests for the difference between the control and treatment groups.
[bloom2024:L203] One possible explanation for the lower quit rates in the treatment group is that quit rates in the control group increased because the individuals in this group were annoyed about being randomized out of the experiment. However, quit rates in the same Airfare and IT divisions were 9.8% in the six months before the experiment—higher than the rate for the control group during the experimental period. Quit rates over the experimental period in the two other Trip.com divisions for which we have data (Business Trips and Marketing) were 10.5% and 9.8%—again higher than that for the control group during the experimental period. This suggests that, if anything, the control-group quit rates were reduced rather than increased by the experiment, possibly because some of them guessed (correctly) that the policy would be rolled out to all employees once the experiment ended.

We saw a significant drop in attrition of 3.3 percentage points for the non-managers, which against a control-group base of 8.6% is a 40% reduction (mean

[bloom2024:L213] P
[bloom2024:L214] = 0.026). By contrast, there was an insignificant increase in attrition for managers (mean
[bloom2024:L215] control
[bloom2024:L216] = 2.96, mean
[bloom2024:L217] treat
[bloom2024:L218] = 3.13,
[bloom2024:L219] t

[bloom2024:L221] P
[bloom2024:L222] = 0.922). We also found that non-managers were more enthusiastic before the experiment, with a volunteering rate of 35% (versus 22% for managers), matching the media sentiment that although non-managerial employees are enthusiastic about WFH, many managers are not (
[bloom2024:L223] t
[bloom2024:L224] (1610) = 4.86,
[bloom2024:L225] P

[bloom2024:L246] Second, we examined the effect on attrition by total commute length, splitting the sample into people with shorter and longer total commutes on the basis of the median commute duration (two-way commutes of 1.5 h or less versus those exceeding 1.5 h, with 648 and 611 employees, respectively). [bloom2024:L271] P
[bloom2024:L272] < 0.001). This is not surprising given that the most frequently cited benefit of WFH is no commute
[bloom2024:L273] 1
[bloom2024:L274] .
[bloom2024:L275] Extended Data Table 3
